# PHASE 1 — FOUNDATIONS

**Korean Roadmap · Levels 0–3 of 18**

> **Phase goal.** Build the substrate everything else sits on: a sound system you can actually produce, a reading system that works on words you have never seen, and the grammar engine that generates sentences instead of storing them. By the end of this phase you read anything aloud correctly, survive a week in Korea without English, conjugate any verb in any tense, and talk about your past for three minutes.

---

## Phase dashboard

| Item | Details |
|---|---|
| **Phase** | 1 — Foundations |
| **Levels** | L0 · L1 · L2 · L3 |
| **Difficulty** | Absolute beginner → lower intermediate |
| **Suggested duration** | 2.5–3.5 months at 2–3 hours/day (see pacing table) |
| **Study commitment** | 2–3 hours per day, 6 days per week; the 7th day is a checkpoint |
| **Core tools** | Anki, a recorder, Naver Dictionary, *Korean Grammar in Use – Beginning*, a tutor from L2 |
| **Capstone projects** | 4 level capstones + 1 phase capstone |
| **Final outcome** | Read at 60+ syllables/min with all sound rules; hold a 3-minute unprepared conversation on your own life; produce 55 grammar patterns; command 700 active words. |

**Prerequisites.** None. If you already read Hangeul, take the placement test at the end of the phase and skip only the levels whose test you pass outright — most self-taught learners pass L0's reading and fail L0's *sound rules*, which is exactly the hole that caps them at survival Korean for years.

| Block | What it contains |

| **Question bank** | Graded questions you should be able to answer after the level |
| **Level assessment** | A checklist before you move on |

> **The rule of this phase:** you do not "know" a pattern until you have produced it out loud, unprompted, in a sentence about your own life. Reading an explanation is preparation for learning, not learning.

# LEVEL 0 — HANGEUL AND THE SOUND SYSTEM

> **Goal.** Read any Korean text aloud correctly — including words you have never seen — with all seven sound-change rules applied without conscious thought. Then produce the sounds well enough that a native speaker does not have to work to understand you.

## Level dashboard

| Item | Details |
|---|---|
| **Level** | 0 — Hangeul and the Sound System |
| **Difficulty** | Beginner, but front-loaded deliberately |
| **Suggested duration** | 2–3 weeks |
| **Study commitment** | 2 hours per day |
| **Core tools** | Anki, a recorder, a native-speaker audio source, a syllable chart |
| **Main project** | **`sound-lab`** — a recorded corpus proving you can read and pronounce |
| **Final outcome** | 60+ syllables/min on unseen text, correct sound rules, and a phoneme inventory you can produce on demand. |

**Prerequisites.** None.

---

## 0.1 — Why Hangeul is a design problem, not a memorisation problem

**Objective.** Understand the logic of the script so that 24 letters take hours instead of weeks.

### Module 0.1.1 — The featural principle

- [ ] Every consonant shape encodes where the sound is made: `ㄱ` the tongue rising at the back; `ㄴ` the tongue at the ridge; `ㅁ` the shape of a mouth; `ㅅ` the shape of teeth; `ㅇ` the throat, empty.
- [ ] Consonants come in families — plain, aspirated, tensed — and the aspiration is drawn *as an extra stroke*: `ㄱ` → `ㅋ`, `ㄷ` → `ㅌ`, `ㅂ` → `ㅍ`, `ㅈ` → `ㅊ`.
- [ ] Vowels are built from three elements: a dot (sky, ㅗ/ㅏ), a horizontal line (earth, ㅡ), a vertical line (human, ㅣ). Every compound vowel is a combination.
- [ ] There are no capitals, no cursive, and no positional letter variants.

> This is why the script is learnable in days. It is one of the few writing systems in the world that a literate adult can acquire analytically rather than by rote.

### Module 0.1.2 — What Hangeul does *not* tell you

- [ ] It is phonemic, not phonetic: it spells the *morpheme*, not the sound. `꽃이` is written with ㅊ and pronounced [꼬치].
- [ ] Sound changes are unwritten. The gap between spelling and speech is the whole difficulty of Korean listening.
- [ ] Spacing (띄어쓰기) carries grammatical information and is a separate skill (L6).

> **Module completion gate (0.1).** You can explain, in two sentences, why Hangeul is fast to learn and why that does not make Korean fast to learn.

## 0.2 — The nineteen consonants

**Objective.** Produce all nineteen, and hear the three-way distinction that English lacks.

### Module 0.2.1 — The fourteen basic consonants

| Letter | Sound | Description |
|---|---|---|
| ㄱ | unaspirated k/g | between English *g* and *k*; no puff of air |
| ㄴ | n | as in *no* |
| ㄷ | unaspirated t/d | between *d* and *t* |
| ㄹ | tapped r / l | Spanish *pero* between vowels; *l* as a final |
| ㅁ | m | |
| ㅂ | unaspirated p/b | between *b* and *p* |
| ㅅ | s | *sh* before ㅣ and ㅑ/ㅕ/ㅛ/ㅠ |
| ㅇ | silent initially; *ng* finally | a placeholder at the start of a syllable |
| ㅈ | unaspirated ch/j | |
| ㅊ | aspirated ch | with a puff |
| ㅋ | aspirated k | with a puff |
| ㅌ | aspirated t | with a puff |
| ㅍ | aspirated p | with a puff |
| ㅎ | h | |

### Module 0.2.2 — The five tense consonants

| Letter | Sound | Production note |
|---|---|---|
| ㄲ | tense k | throat tight, no air |
| ㄸ | tense t | |
| ㅃ | tense p | |
| ㅆ | tense s | hissy, sustained |
| ㅉ | tense j | |

### Module 0.2.3 — The three-way stop distinction

- [ ] English has a two-way distinction (voiced/voiceless). Korean has three: **plain** (ㄱ), **aspirated** (ㅋ), **tensed** (ㄲ).
- [ ] Say the triplet `가 / 카 / 까` until a native speaker or a recording confirms all three are distinct.
- [ ] Do not rely on voicing ([g] vs [k]) — Korean's plain series is voiced between vowels and voiceless elsewhere, which is not a distinction English ears track.
- [ ] Hold a sheet of paper in front of your mouth: ㅋ and ㅌ should move it, ㄱ and ㄲ should not, and ㄲ should feel tighter in the throat than ㄱ.

> **Why this matters now.** If you cannot distinguish these three at the start, you cannot hear them later — and Korean is full of minimal pairs that depend on them: `불` fire / `풀` grass / `뿔` horn.

> **Module completion gate (0.2).** You produce all nineteen consonants on demand from a random order, and a recording of yourself saying 가/카/까, 다/타/따, 바/파/빠 is audibly three-way different.

## 0.3 — The twenty-one vowels

**Objective.** Produce ten basic and eleven compound vowels without collapsing ㅓ/ㅗ or ㅡ/ㅜ.

### Module 0.3.1 — The ten basic vowels

| Letter | Sound | Anchor |
|---|---|---|
| ㅏ | a | *father*, short |
| ㅑ | ya | *yard* |
| ㅓ | eo | *uh*, mouth open, tongue back — **not** *oh* |
| ㅕ | yeo | *yuh* |
| ㅗ | o | *go*, rounded |
| ㅛ | yo | |
| ㅜ | u | *boot*, short |
| ㅠ | yu | |
| ㅡ | eu | teeth together, lips flat, a grunt — **not** *oo* |
| ㅣ | i | *see*, short |

### Module 0.3.2 — The eleven compound vowels

| Letter | Sound | Note |
|---|---|---|
| ㅐ | ae | *bed*, open |
| ㅒ | yae | rare |
| ㅔ | e | *bed*, shorter; merged with ㅐ for most speakers under 50 |
| ㅖ | ye | |
| ㅘ | wa | |
| ㅙ | wae | |
| ㅚ | oe | *way* in modern Seoul speech |
| ㅝ | wo | |
| ㅞ | we | |
| ㅟ | wi | |
| ㅢ | ui | glide; as the possessive particle it is pronounced 에 |

### Module 0.3.3 — The two collapses to fix now

- [ ] **ㅓ vs ㅗ.** English speakers round ㅓ into ㅗ. Say pairs: 어-오, 어-오. Then `언어` (language) — it must not become 오노.
- [ ] **ㅡ vs ㅜ.** ㅡ is unrounded; ㅜ is rounded. Say pairs: 크-쿠, 크-쿠. Then `그` (that) versus `구` (nine).
- [ ] Record both pairs and listen back with headphones. This self-check catches what your ear will not.

> **Module completion gate (0.3).** You read the full vowel chart from memory, and your recording of 어/오 and 크/쿠 is distinguishable by a native speaker or by a language-exchange partner.

## 0.4 — Syllable blocks and writing

**Objective.** Build and handwrite any syllable.

### Module 0.4.1 — Block structure

- [ ] Every syllable is `(initial consonant) + vowel + (final consonant)`.
- [ ] Six shapes: `가` (C+V), `강` (C+V+C), `과` (C+V+V), `아` (ㅇ+V), `안` (ㅇ+V+C), `완` (C+V+V+C).
- [ ] ㅇ is silent in the initial position and *ng* in the final position.
- [ ] Stroke order: top-left first, then right, then bottom. Learn it once, correctly.

### Module 0.4.2 — Handwriting

- [ ] Write each of the 40 letters ten times, saying the sound aloud.
- [ ] Write your own name in Hangeul, then a Koreanised version of three friends' names.
- [ ] Write ten words from dictation given by a text-to-speech voice.

> **Module completion gate (0.4).** You handwrite 20 random syllables from dictation with correct block construction and legible stroke order.

## 0.5 — Batchim and the seven-sound rule

**Objective.** Understand why written Korean has 27 final consonants and spoken Korean has seven.

### Module 0.5.1 — The collapse

| Spoken as | Written letters that collapse into it | Example |
|---|---|---|
| **ㄱ** [k] | ㄱ ㄲ ㅋ ㄳ ㄺ | 국 [국], 밖 [박], 부엌 [부억] |
| **ㄴ** [n] | ㄴ ㄵ ㄶ | 눈 [눈], 앉다 [안따] |
| **ㄷ** [t] | ㄷ ㅅ ㅆ ㅈ ㅊ ㅌ ㅎ | 옷 [옫], 꽃 [꼳], 낮 [낟], 있다 [읻따] |
| **ㄹ** [l] | ㄹ ㄼ ㄽ ㄾ ㅀ | 말 [말], 여덟 [여덜] |
| **ㅁ** [m] | ㅁ ㄻ | 밤 [밤], 삶 [삼] |
| **ㅂ** [p] | ㅂ ㅍ ㅄ ㄿ | 밥 [밥], 앞 [압], 값 [갑] |
| **ㅇ** [ng] | ㅇ | 강 [강] |

### Module 0.5.2 — Double batchim: one survives

- [ ] ㄺ → ㄱ before a consonant: 읽다 [익따], 맑다 [막따], 닭 [닥]
- [ ] ㄻ → ㅁ: 삶 [삼]
- [ ] ㄼ → ㄹ: 여덟 [여덜], 넓다 [널따]
- [ ] ㅄ → ㅂ: 값 [갑], 없어요 [업써요]
- [ ] ㄵ → ㄴ: 앉아요 [안자요]
- [ ] ㄶ → ㄴ, with the ㅎ dropped: 많아요 [마나요]

### Module 0.5.3 — Unreleased finals

- [ ] A final stop (ㄱ/ㄷ/ㅂ) is never released in Korean — no puff of air at the end. `밥` ends with the lips closed, not with a *p*.
- [ ] This is the single most audible marker of a foreign accent. Practise stopping the sound in your mouth.

> **Module completion gate (0.5).** Given any batchim word, you state its seven-sound collapse without a reference, and your recording of 밥/밤/밥 does not release the final.

## 0.6 — The seven sound-change rules

**Objective.** Turn written Korean into spoken Korean. This section is the reason your reading will sound human.

### Module 0.6.1 — Liaison (연음): the final moves forward

$$\text{C}_{\text{final}} + \varnothing_{\text{onset}} \;\longrightarrow\; \varnothing_{\text{final}} + \text{C}_{\text{onset}}$$

| Written | Spoken | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| 한국어 | [한구거] | Korean language |
| 음악 | [으막] | music |
| 옷이 | [오시] | clothes (subject) |
| 이름은 | [이르믄] | name (topic) |
| 먹어요 | [머거요] | eats |
| 좋아요 | [조아요] | good |

- [ ] This is the most frequent rule in the language. At normal speed, words bleed into each other constantly.
- [ ] Drill: read 30 two-syllable words where the second begins with ㅇ, applying liaison every time.

### Module 0.6.2 — Aspiration (격음화): ㅎ fuses with a stop

| Combination | Becomes | Example |
|---|---|---|
| ㄱ + ㅎ / ㅎ + ㄱ | ㅋ | 놓고 [노코], 축하 [추카] |
| ㄷ + ㅎ | ㅌ | 좋다 [조타], 맏형 [마텽] |
| ㅂ + ㅎ | ㅍ | 잡히다 [자피다] |
| ㅈ + ㅎ | ㅊ | 앉히다 [안치다] |

### Module 0.6.3 — Tensification (경음화): after a stop batchim

ㄱ/ㄷ/ㅂ + ㄱ/ㄷ/ㅂ/ㅅ/ㅈ → tensed.

| Written | Spoken | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| 학교 | [학꾜] | school |
| 먹다 | [먹따] | to eat |
| 잡지 | [잡찌] | magazine |
| 국밥 | [국빱] | rice in soup |
| 있다 | [읻따] | to exist |
| 식당 | [식땅] | restaurant |

### Module 0.6.4 — Nasalisation (비음화): stop + nasal

ㄱ/ㄷ/ㅂ + ㄴ/ㅁ → ㅇ/ㄴ/ㅁ.

| Written | Spoken | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| 국물 | [궁물] | broth |
| 닫는 | [단는] | closing |
| 밥물 | [밤물] | rice water |
| 한국말 | [한궁말] | Korean speech |
| 십만 | [심만] | one hundred thousand |
| 있습니다 | [이씀니다] | there is |

### Module 0.6.5 — Liquidisation (유음화): ㄴ and ㄹ

ㄴ + ㄹ or ㄹ + ㄴ → [ㄹㄹ].

| Written | Spoken | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| 신라 | [실라] | Silla |
| 연락 | [열락] | contact |
| 한류 | [할류] | Korean wave |
| 물냉면 | [물랭면] | cold noodles in broth |
| 설날 | [설랄] | Lunar New Year |

### Module 0.6.6 — Palatalisation (구개음화): ㄷ/ㅌ before 이

| Written | Spoken | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| 같이 | [가치] | together |
| 굳이 | [구지] | stubbornly |
| 밭이 | [바치] | field (subject) |
| 붙이다 | [부치다] | to attach |
| 해돋이 | [해도지] | sunrise |

### Module 0.6.7 — ㅎ deletion

| Written | Spoken | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| 싫어요 | [시러요] | I don't like it |
| 귀찮아요 | [귀차나요] | it's bothersome |
| 괜찮아요 | [괜차나요] | it's fine |
| 않아요 | [아나요] | does not |
| 전화 | [저놔] | telephone |
| 은행 | [으냉] | bank |

> **The sentence that identifies a beginner.** `괜찮아요` said as *gwaen-chan-a-yo*, with a full ㅎ, is understood — and immediately placed. Say [괜차나요]. Record yourself until it sounds like one word, not four syllables.

> **Module completion gate (0.6).** You read 20 unseen words containing sound changes, state which rule applies to each, and your spoken version matches the rule. You can list all seven rules from memory.

## 0.7 — Prosody: rhythm, pitch, and intonation

**Objective.** Stop sounding like a sequence of syllables. Korean has no stress accent, so learners who import English stress sound wrong even when every phoneme is correct.

### Module 0.7.1 — Syllable timing

- [ ] Korean is syllable-timed, not stress-timed. Every syllable gets roughly equal duration; do not crush unstressed syllables the way English does.
- [ ] Consequence: Korean speech sounds *flatter and faster* than English to a learner, and your English rhythm sounds *lumpy* to a Korean.

### Module 0.7.2 — Pitch

- [ ] Korean uses pitch at the phrase level, not the word level: a rise at the end of a question, a fall at the end of a statement.
- [ ] Long vowels carry emotional weight: `네~` (yes, drawn out) signals hesitation or disbelief.
- [ ] There is no lexical tone — pitch does not change word meaning, but it changes *attitude*, which is where misunderstanding lives.

### Module 0.7.3 — Intonation contours to copy

| Function | Contour | Example |
|---|---|---|
| Statement | level then slight fall | 갔어요. |
| Yes/no question | rise at the end | 갔어요? |
| Wh-question | rise then fall | 어디 갔어요? |
| Surprise | high rise | 정말요? |
| Listing | slight rise on each, fall on last | 사과, 배, 감. |

> **Module completion gate (0.7).** You shadow a 60-second native clip and your recording has the same contour shape — checked visually on a pitch track or by comparing lengths of matched phrases.

## 0.8 — Reading fluency

**Objective.** Convert decoding into reading. Accuracy first, then speed.

### Module 0.8.1 — Measurement

$$\text{syllables per minute} = \frac{\text{syllables read}}{\text{seconds}} \times 60$$

- [ ] Take a 100-syllable passage, read aloud, time it, divide.
- [ ] Count errors separately. A fast read with wrong sound rules is not progress.

| Stage | Speed target | Accuracy target |
|---|---|---|
| End of 0.4 | 20 syl/min | 90% |
| End of 0.6 | 40 syl/min | 95% |
| End of L0 | 60 syl/min | 98% |
| L6 | 80 syl/min | 99% |
| L12 | 110 syl/min | 99% |
| L16 | 130+ syl/min | 99% |

### Module 0.8.2 — What to read

- [ ] Start with syllable charts, then word lists, then short sentences, then a paragraph of a children's book.
- [ ] Read *novel* text every session — re-reading the same passage inflates speed without building decoding.
- [ ] Read aloud, always. Silent reading at this stage trains nothing.

> **Module completion gate (0.8).** 60 syllables per minute on unseen text at 98% rule accuracy, measured three days in a row.

## 0.9 — Listening for phonemes

**Objective.** Build the perceptual categories that reading cannot build. This module is why your pronunciation will improve without a tutor in this level.

### Module 0.9.1 — Minimal-pair discrimination

| Pair | Contrast |
|---|---|
| 가 / 카 / 까 | plain / aspirated / tensed |
| 다 / 타 / 따 | |
| 바 / 파 / 빠 | |
| 자 / 차 / 짜 | |
| 살 / 쌀 | plain / tensed s |
| 어 / 오 | vowel collapse |
| 크 / 쿠 | unrounded / rounded |
| 눈 / 눋 | none — both [눈]; a spelling lesson |

### Module 0.9.2 — Dictation as a test of perception

- [ ] Dictate 20 words per session from an audio source, in Hangeul.
- [ ] Mark every miss. Group the misses: was it a consonant, a vowel, a batchim, or a sound rule?
- [ ] The group with the most misses is your training target for the next session.

### Module 0.9.3 — The three-pass method

- [ ] Pass 1 blind; pass 2 sentence-by-sentence dictation; pass 3 compare and mark.
- [ ] Re-listen to the missed spots immediately, three times, before moving on.

> **Module completion gate (0.9).** 20-word dictation at 95% accuracy, with misses classified by cause rather than simply corrected.

## 0.10 — Level 0 Capstone: `sound-lab`

**Objective.** Produce a recorded, reviewable corpus that proves the level's outcome — not a claim that you "know the alphabet".

### A. Functional requirements

1. **The alphabet corpus.** You record all 40 letters, isolated, in a single take, with no reading from a list you can see mid-recording.
2. **The minimal-pair corpus.** Ten triplets (가/카/까 and nine others) recorded twice, with a 2-second gap.
3. **The sound-rule corpus.** Twenty sentences, each containing at least one sound-change rule, recorded at natural speed.
4. **The reading corpus.** Three unseen passages of 100+ syllables, read aloud, timed, with the time and syllable count written down.
5. **The dictation log.** Ten sessions of 20 words, with accuracy per session and a running list of your recurring phoneme errors.
6. **The self-assessment.** One paragraph, written, naming your three weakest sounds and the drill you used for each.

### B. Suggested architecture

```
sound-lab/
│
├── 01_alphabet/            ← 40 isolated letters, one take
│   └── alphabet.wav
│
├── 02_minimal_pairs/       ← triplets, twice each
│   └── stops_triplets.wav
│
├── 03_rules/               ← 20 sentences, natural speed
│   ├── liaison.txt         ← the sentence, the rule, the expected IPA-ish gloss
│   ├── tensification.txt
│   ├── nasalisation.txt
│   └── rules.wav
│
├── 04_reading/             ← three unseen passages + timings
│   ├── passage_1.txt
│   ├── record_1.wav
│   └── timings.md
│
├── 05_dictation/           ← ten sessions, accuracy per session
│   └── log.md
│
└── 06_review/
    ├── self_assessment.md  ← three weakest sounds + the drills used
    └── reviewer_notes.md   ← filled by a tutor or exchange partner
```

### C. Suggested folder discipline

| File | Must contain |
|---|---|
| `timings.md` | date, passage length, seconds, syllables/min, error count |
| `log.md` | session number, source clip, accuracy, error category |
| `self_assessment.md` | three specific weaknesses, not "pronunciation" |
| `reviewer_notes.md` | one sentence per corpus item from a native or advanced speaker |

### D. Acceptance targets

| Dimension | Target |
|---|---|
| Reading speed | ≥ 60 syllables/minute on unseen text |
| Rule accuracy | ≥ 98% on 50 randomly sampled syllables |
| Covering all rules | at least one example of each of the seven rules in the corpus |
| Dictation | ≥ 95% over the last three sessions |
| Native intelligibility | a native speaker transcribes 18 of 20 rule sentences correctly |
| Self-assessment honesty | names three specific phonemes, not generalities |

### E. Stretch goals

- Record the corpus a second time on a different day and compare — consistency is the real test.
- Read a nursery rhyme or a poem aloud with correct rhythm, not just correct phonemes.
- Have a native speaker produce the same minimal-pair corpus and compare yours side by side.

### F. Acceptance criteria table

| Requirement | Weight | Pass condition | Evidence |
|---|---|---|---|
| Alphabet corpus complete | required | all 40 letters, one take | `01_alphabet/` |
| Seven rules exemplified | required | ≥ 1 example each, correctly applied | `03_rules/` |
| Reading speed | required | ≥ 60 syl/min on 3 passages | `04_reading/timings.md` |
| Rule accuracy | required | ≥ 98% | sampled by reviewer |
| Dictation | required | ≥ 95% over last 3 sessions | `05_dictation/log.md` |
| Native intelligibility | required | 18/20 sentences transcribed correctly | `reviewer_notes.md` |
| Self-assessment | required | three specific phonemes named | `06_review/self_assessment.md` |
| Consistency | stretch | second recording within 5% of the first | `06_review/` |

**Rule:** all *required* rows must pass. A capstone with a 90% reading accuracy but a complete corpus does not pass — the corpus is the proof, so the accuracy has to be there too.

---

## Level 0 question bank

### Group A — Reading and orthography

1. Read aloud: `안녕하세요. 저는 인도 사람입니다.`
2. Read aloud and state which rule applies: `한국어, 좋아요, 있습니다, 같이, 신라, 국밥, 괜찮아요.`
3. Why is `꽃` pronounced [꼳]?
4. Why does `밥` not release its final consonant?
5. What are the seven sounds that batchim can produce, and which letters map to each?
6. Write the Hangeul for *Haneul* (sky). Now for *Hanguk* (Korea).
7. Which of these is written with a batchim: 가, 강, 고, 구?
8. Explain why `한국어` is three syllables written and four sounds spoken.

### Group B — Production

9. Say the three-way distinction for ㄱ, ㄷ, ㅂ, ㅈ.
10. Say these vowels in order: ㅏ ㅓ ㅗ ㅜ ㅡ ㅣ, without collapsing ㅓ/ㅗ or ㅡ/ㅜ.
11. Say the Korean for the numbers 1–10 in Sino-Korean, then in native Korean.
12. Say `괜찮아요` the way it is actually spoken.
13. Say a four-syllable word with a tensed consonant three times.

### Group C — Listening

14. Dictate ten words from audio, no transcript.
15. Which word did you hear: 살 or 쌀? 어 or 오?
16. In a native clip, locate three words where liaison occurred.

### Group D — Metalanguage

17. What is liaison, in one sentence?
18. Why does Korean spelling not match Korean speech?
19. What is the difference between a batchim and a final consonant cluster?
20. Name the two vowels that English speakers most often collapse, and say why.

### Readiness standard

| Group | Requirement |
|---|---|
| A | 8/8, with the rule named for each example |
| B | all five produced correctly to a native or near-native listener |
| C | 8/10 on dictation, with errors classified |
| D | all four answered in full sentences |

**If Group B fails, the level is not complete**, regardless of how well the reading went. Reading is decoding; speech is the skill.

---

## Level 0 assessment

**Knowledge**

- [ ] The featural logic of the script, in two sentences.
- [ ] The seven batchim sounds and their letter mappings.
- [ ] The seven sound-change rules, named and exemplified.

**Practical skills**

- [ ] 60+ syllables/minute on unseen text.
- [ ] 98% rule accuracy on sampled text.
- [ ] 95% dictation on the last three sessions.
- [ ] Native intelligibility confirmed on 20 sentences.

**Project**

- [ ] `sound-lab` complete with all required rows passing.

> **Level 0 completion rule.** If you can read quickly but a native speaker cannot transcribe your speech, you have completed a decoding course, not this level. Repeat 0.6–0.9 until the corpus passes.

---

# LEVEL 1 — BEGINNER KOREAN (ABSOLUTE BEGINNER → BASIC COMMUNICATION)

> **Goal.** Master Level 1 completely following the university standard (SNU Korean+ inspired 5-step pedagogy: 대화 → 어휘 → 문법 → 말하기·듣기 → 읽기·쓰기). By the end, communicate smoothly across all 16 units: read/write Hangul, introduce yourself, order food/drinks, buy items with counters and Won, describe daily routines, make appointments, talk about past experiences, navigate public transit, and deliver connected life narratives.

## Level dashboard

| Item | Details |
|---|---|
| **Level** | 1 — Beginner Korean (Parts A, B, C, D) |
| **Difficulty** | Beginner (CEFR A1 / TOPIK 1 Level 1) |
| **Suggested duration** | 6–8 weeks |
| **Study commitment** | 1.5–2 hours per day |
| **Pedagogical Structure** | Level 1 → 4 Parts → 16 Units → 34 Structured 5-Step Lessons |
| **Core Method** | 5-Step Lesson Flow: 1. 대화 (Conversation) → 2. 어휘 (Vocabulary) → 3. 문법 (Grammar) → 4. 말하기·듣기 (Speaking & Listening) → 5. 읽기·쓰기 (Reading & Writing) |
| **Main project** | **`korean-level1-mastery`** — Complete spoken simulation + written essay across all 4 Checkpoints (A, B, C, D) |
| **Final outcome** | Confident everyday survival fluency across 16 real-world situational domains with native audio drills. |

**Prerequisites.** Level 0 complete (Hangul fundamentals).

## Unit 0 — Hangul: The Alphabet & Sound System (한글과 발음 기초)

**Part.** Part 0 — Hangul Foundations (Unit 0)
**Objective.** Master basic vowels, consonants, compound letters, and syllable codas (받침) to decouple completely from romanization.

### 0.1 — Meeting Korean Letters: Vowels, Consonants & Syllable Blocks (기본 모음과 기본 자음)

**Situation & Objective.** Understanding the featural design of King Sejong's alphabet and forming two-letter syllable blocks.

#### Module 0.1.1 — 대화 (Conversation First)

- [ ] **Dialogue Practice:** Listen to and practice the dialogue between 선생님 and 학생 and 선생님.
  - `한글 모음 "ㅏ, ㅓ, ㅗ, ㅜ"를 소리 내어 읽어 보세요.` (Try reading the Hangul vowels "a, eo, o, u" aloud.) — *Note: Bright vowels (ㅏ, ㅗ) vs Dark vowels (ㅓ, ㅜ).*
  - `ㅏ, ㅓ, ㅗ, ㅜ. 자음 "ㄱ, ㄴ, ㄷ"도 같이 읽어요?` (a, eo, o, u. Shall we also read consonants "g, n, d" together?)
  - `네, "가, 나, 다"처럼 모음과 합쳐서 소리를 만듭니다.` (Yes, just like "ga, na, da", combine consonants with vowels to create sounds.)
- [ ] **Cultural Etiquette & Pragmatics:** Hangul letters are never written in a linear row like English (n-a-m-u); they are stacked into syllable blocks representing CV or CVC sounds (나무).

#### Module 0.1.2 — 어휘 (Vocabulary in Context)

- [ ] **Core Target Words:** Memorise and pronounce all target words for this lesson:
  - [ ] `한글` (han-geul) [Noun]: Hangeul (Korean alphabet) — e.g., "한글은 읽기 쉬운 글자입니다." (*Hangeul is an easy-to-read script.*)
  - [ ] `모음` (mo-eum, Hanja: 母音) [Noun]: Vowel (literally "mother sound") — e.g., "기본 모음은 열 자입니다." (*There are ten basic vowels.*)
  - [ ] `자음` (ja-eum, Hanja: 子音) [Noun]: Consonant (literally "child sound") — e.g., "기본 자음은 14자입니다." (*There are 14 basic consonants.*)
  - [ ] `나무` (na-mu) [Noun]: Tree / wood — e.g., "공원에 큰 나무가 있어요." (*There is a big tree in the park.*)
  - [ ] `아이` (a-i) [Noun]: Child / baby — e.g., "아이가 활짝 웃어요." (*The child smiles brightly.*)

#### Module 0.1.3 — 문법 (Grammar Formulas & Rules)

- [ ] **Grammar Pattern: `Syllable Block Formation (음절 블록 구조)`** (Combining Initial Consonant (초성) + Medial Vowel (중성))
  - Formula: `Initial Consonant (C) + Medial Vowel (V)`
  - Explanation: Every Korean syllable block MUST begin with a consonant letter. When a syllable begins with a vowel sound, the silent placeholder consonant "ㅇ" must be placed in the initial position (e.g., 아, 오, 우).
  - Rule: Vertical vowels (ㅏ, ㅑ, ㅓ, ㅕ, ㅣ) place the consonant on the left: ㄱ + ㅏ = 가.
  - Rule: Horizontal vowels (ㅗ, ㅛ, ㅜ, ㅠ, ㅡ) place the consonant on top: ㄱ + ㅗ = 고.
  - Rule: Silent "ㅇ" has no sound value at the start of a syllable: ㅇ + ㅣ = 이 (pronounced "ee").
  - Example: `가, 나, 다, 라, 마, 바, 사` — *ga, na, da, ra, ma, ba, sa*
  - Example: `고, 노, 도, 로, 모, 보, 소` — *go, no, do, ro, mo, bo, so*
  - Example: `우유, 오이, 모자, 바지` — *milk, cucumber, hat, pants*

#### Module 0.1.4 — 말하기·듣기 (Shadowing & Drills)

- [ ] **Shadowing & Repetition Drill:** Listen carefully to the contrast between bright and dark vowels, then repeat aloud.
  - [ ] Prompt: "Read vowel pair: a vs eo" → Speak aloud: `아이 / 어디` (*child / where*)
  - [ ] Prompt: "Read vowel pair: o vs u" → Speak aloud: `오이 / 우유` (*cucumber / milk*)
  - [ ] Prompt: "Read consonant contrast: g vs n" → Speak aloud: `가구 / 나라` (*furniture / country*)

#### Module 0.1.5 — 읽기·쓰기 (Reading & Production)

- [ ] **Reading Passage:** "First Korean Syllables"
  - Text: `아이. 오이. 나무. 모자. 다리. 비누. 고기.`
  - Translation: *Child. Cucumber. Tree. Hat. Bridge/leg. Soap. Meat.*
  - [ ] Check: Which word means "tree"? → Answer: `나무 (na-mu)` (*ㄴ + ㅏ = 나, ㅁ + ㅜ = 무.*)
  - [ ] Check: Which word means "cucumber"? → Answer: `오이 (o-i)` (*ㅇ + ㅗ = 오, ㅇ + ㅣ = 이.*)
- [ ] **Guided Writing Task:** Write the 4 syllable blocks for "cow" (소), "tree" (나무), "water" (물), and "shoes" (구두).
  - Sample Model Answer: `소, 나무, 물, 구두`

### 0.2 — Aspirated & Tense Consonants & Compound Vowels (거센소리와 된소리, 이중 모음)

**Situation & Objective.** Distinguishing plain, aspirated, and tense sounds that change word meanings in Korean.

#### Module 0.2.1 — 대화 (Conversation First)

- [ ] **Dialogue Practice:** Listen to and practice the dialogue between 선생님 and 학생 and 선생님.
  - `"달", "탈", "딸"의 소리 차이를 들어 보세요.` (Listen to the sound differences between "dal" (moon), "tal" (mask), and "ttal" (daughter).)
  - `"탈"은 공기가 많이 나오고, "딸"은 목에 힘이 들어가네요.` ("Tal" has a lot of air coming out, and "ttal" has tension in the throat.)
  - `정확해요! 한국어에서는 이 세 소리가 완전히 다른 단어입니다.` (Exactly! In Korean, these three sounds create completely different words.)
- [ ] **Cultural Etiquette & Pragmatics:** Korean does not distinguish voiced/voiceless sounds (B vs P) as phonemes; it distinguishes three-way phonation: Plain (ㄱ), Aspirated (ㅋ - puff of air), and Tense (ㄲ - tight vocal cords).

#### Module 0.2.2 — 어휘 (Vocabulary in Context)

- [ ] **Core Target Words:** Memorise and pronounce all target words for this lesson:
  - [ ] `달` (dal) [Noun]: Moon / month — e.g., "밤하늘에 밝은 달이 떴어요." (*A bright moon rose in the night sky.*)
  - [ ] `탈` (tal) [Noun]: Mask (traditional) — e.g., "전통 하회탈을 썼습니다." (*I put on a traditional Hahoe mask.*)
  - [ ] `딸` (ttal) [Noun]: Daughter — e.g., "우리 딸은 일곱 살입니다." (*Our daughter is seven years old.*)
  - [ ] `사과` (sa-gwa) [Noun]: Apple / apology — e.g., "달콤한 사과를 씻었어요." (*I washed a sweet apple.*)
  - [ ] `회사` (hoe-sa, Hanja: 會社) [Noun]: Company / office — e.g., "내일 아침에 회사에 갑니다." (*I am going to the company tomorrow morning.*)

#### Module 0.2.3 — 문법 (Grammar Formulas & Rules)

- [ ] **Grammar Pattern: `The 3-Way Consonant Phonation Matrix (평음 · 격음 · 된소리)`** (Plain vs Aspirated vs Tense Consonants)
  - Formula: `Plain (ㄱ, ㄷ, ㅂ, ㅅ, ㅈ) ↔ Aspirated (ㅋ, ㅌ, ㅍ, —, ㅊ) ↔ Tense (ㄲ, ㄸ, ㅃ, ㅆ, ㅉ)`
  - Explanation: Placing a hand or sheet of paper in front of your mouth reveals the difference: Plain causes slight air, Aspirated blows the paper vigorously, and Tense emits almost zero air while tensing the glottis.
  - Rule: 불 (fire - plain) vs 풀 (grass - aspirated) vs 뿔 (horn - tense).
  - Rule: 방 (room - plain) vs 빵 (bread - tense).
  - Rule: 자다 (to sleep - plain) vs 차다 (to kick - aspirated) vs 짜다 (to be salty - tense).
  - Example: `방 / 빵` — *room / bread*
  - Example: `자다 / 차다 / 짜다` — *to sleep / to kick / to be salty*
  - Example: `가다 / 카드 / 까다` — *to go / card / to peel*

#### Module 0.2.4 — 말하기·듣기 (Shadowing & Drills)

- [ ] **Shadowing & Repetition Drill:** Listen and distinguish plain vs aspirated vs tense consonants.
  - [ ] Prompt: "Distinguish: bang vs ppang" → Speak aloud: `방 / 빵` (*room / bread*)
  - [ ] Prompt: "Distinguish: dal vs tal vs ttal" → Speak aloud: `달 / 탈 / 딸` (*moon / mask / daughter*)
  - [ ] Prompt: "Distinguish: jada vs chada vs jjada" → Speak aloud: `자다 / 차다 / 짜다` (*sleep / kick / salty*)

#### Module 0.2.5 — 읽기·쓰기 (Reading & Production)

- [ ] **Reading Passage:** "Consonant Distinctions Passage"
  - Text: `아침에 빵을 먹어요. 시원한 차를 마셔요. 날씨가 조금 추워요. 회사에 가요.`
  - Translation: *I eat bread in the morning. I drink cool tea. The weather is a bit cold. I go to the office.*
  - [ ] Check: What does the speaker eat? → Answer: `빵 (bread)` (*Tense consonant ㅃ in 빵.*)
  - [ ] Check: Where is the speaker going? → Answer: `회사 (company/office)` (*Compound vowel ㅚ in 회.*)
- [ ] **Guided Writing Task:** Write down the contrasting pairs: "room" (방) and "bread" (빵); "foot" (발) and "arm" (팔).
  - Sample Model Answer: `방 - 빵 / 발 - 팔`

### 0.3 — Final Consonants (받침) & The 7 Sound-Change Rules (받침과 7개 대표음, 연음 규칙)

**Situation & Objective.** Mastering syllable codas and smooth liaison when reading words in sequence.

#### Module 0.3.1 — 대화 (Conversation First)

- [ ] **Dialogue Practice:** Listen to and practice the dialogue between 안내원 and 승객.
  - `출구 쪽에 "꽃집 [꼳찝]" 간판이 보이세요?` (Do you see the "flower shop [kkot-jjip]" sign by the exit?) — *Note: 꽃 [꼳] neutralizes to [ㄷ], causing tensification on 집 [찝].*
  - `네, 보여요! "한국어 [한구거]" 책도 거기서 파나요?` (Yes, I see it! Do they also sell "Korean [han-gu-geo]" books there?) — *Note: 한국어 undergoes liaison: ㄱ moves to empty ㅇ space -> [한구거].*
- [ ] **Cultural Etiquette & Pragmatics:** Although 27 different consonants and double-consonant clusters can be written as 받침, they can only make 7 phonetic sounds at the end of a syllable: [ㄱ, ㄴ, ㄷ, ㄹ, ㅁ, ㅂ, ㅇ].

#### Module 0.3.2 — 어휘 (Vocabulary in Context)

- [ ] **Core Target Words:** Memorise and pronounce all target words for this lesson:
  - [ ] `꽃` (kkot) [Noun]: Flower (pronounced [꼳]) — e.g., "장미꽃 [장미꼳] 이 참 예뻐요." (*The rose flower is very pretty.*)
  - [ ] `한국어` (han-gu-geo, Hanja: 韓國語) [Noun]: Korean language (pronounced [한구거]) — e.g., "한국어를 열심히 공부해요." (*I study Korean diligently.*)
  - [ ] `감사합니다` (gam-sa-ham-ni-da, Hanja: 感謝합니다) [Expression]: Thank you (pronounced [감사함니다]) — e.g., "도와주셔서 감사합니다." (*Thank you for helping me.*)
  - [ ] `책` (chaek, Hanja: 冊) [Noun]: Book — e.g., "책을 매일 읽어요." (*I read books every day.*)
  - [ ] `지하철` (ji-ha-cheol, Hanja: 地下鐵) [Noun]: Subway (pronounced [지하철]) — e.g., "지하철을 타고 학교에 가요." (*I take the subway to school.*)

#### Module 0.3.3 — 문법 (Grammar Formulas & Rules)

- [ ] **Grammar Pattern: `Liaison Rule (연음 법칙)`** (Carrying Syllable-Final Consonants into Vowel Placeholders)
  - Formula: `Syllable with 받침 + Syllable starting with ㅇ → 받침 sound moves to ㅇ`
  - Explanation: When a syllable with a final consonant is immediately followed by a syllable starting with the silent placeholder consonant "ㅇ", the final consonant naturally leaps over to become the initial sound of the next syllable.
  - Rule: 한국어 [한구거]: ㄱ carries over into 어.
  - Rule: 음악 [으막]: ㅁ carries over into 악.
  - Rule: 집에 [지베]: ㅂ carries over into 에.
  - Example: `한국어 → [한구거]` — *Korean language*
  - Example: `음악 → [으막]` — *music*
  - Example: `밥을 먹어요 → [바블 머거요]` — *eat rice*
- [ ] **Grammar Pattern: `Syllable-Final Neutralization (음절의 끝소리 규칙)`** (7 Representative Coda Sounds)
  - Formula: `All codas reduce to: [ㄱ, ㄴ, ㄷ, ㄹ, ㅁ, ㅂ, ㅇ]`
  - Explanation: In isolation or before a consonant, no matter how complex the written coda is, it collapses into one of the 7 sounds. In particular: ㅅ, ㅆ, ㅈ, ㅊ, ㅌ, ㅎ all neutralize to phonetic [ㄷ].
  - Rule: 옷 [옫] (clothes), 꽃 [꼳] (flower), 낮 [낟] (daytime), 빛 [빋] (light) all end in phonetic [ㄷ].
  - Rule: 앞 [압] (front), 잎 [입] (leaf) neutralize to phonetic [ㅂ].
  - Rule: 부엌 [부억] (kitchen) neutralizes to phonetic [ㄱ].
  - Example: `옷 [옫], 꽃 [꼳], 빛 [빋]` — *clothes, flower, light -> all [ㄷ]*
  - Example: `앞 [압], 숲 [숩]` — *front, forest -> all [ㅂ]*

#### Module 0.3.4 — 말하기·듣기 (Shadowing & Drills)

- [ ] **Shadowing & Repetition Drill:** Practice moving final consonants into the following vowel smoothly.
  - [ ] Prompt: "Liaison: ba-beul meo-geo-yo" → Speak aloud: `밥을 먹어요 [바블 머거요]` (*eat a meal*)
  - [ ] Prompt: "Liaison: ha-neul-e" → Speak aloud: `하늘에 [하느레]` (*in the sky*)
  - [ ] Prompt: "Neutralization: kkot-jip" → Speak aloud: `꽃집 [꼳찝]` (*flower shop*)

#### Module 0.3.5 — 읽기·쓰기 (Reading & Production)

- [ ] **Reading Passage:** "Reading Seoul Subway Signs"
  - Text: `서울역 1번 출구입니다. 오른쪽으로 가면 꽃집과 약국이 있습니다. 환승역은 시청역입니다.`
  - Translation: *It is Exit 1 of Seoul Station. If you go right, there is a flower shop and a pharmacy. The transfer station is City Hall Station.*
  - [ ] Check: How is "꽃집" pronounced? → Answer: `[꼳찝]` (*꽃 neutralizes to [ㄷ], triggering tensification [찝].*)
  - [ ] Check: Which exit is mentioned? → Answer: `1번 출구 (Exit 1)` (*일 번 출구.*)
- [ ] **Guided Writing Task:** Transcribe the spoken sounds of: 1) 한국어, 2) 음악, 3) 밥을.
  - Sample Model Answer: `1) [한구거], 2) [으막], 3) [바블]`


## Unit 1 — Unit 1: Greetings & Self-Introductions (제1과: 인사와 자기소개)

**Part.** Part A — Learn to Read and Start Speaking (Units 1–3 & Checkpoint A)
**Objective.** Learn natural greetings, meeting etiquette, and how to introduce your name, nationality, and profession using 이에요/예요 and 은/는.

### 1.1 — Meeting Someone for the First Time: Greetings & Farewells (처음 만났을 때의 인사)

**Situation & Objective.** Meeting a new language exchange partner or acquaintance in Seoul.

#### Module 1.1.1 — 대화 (Conversation First)

- [ ] **Dialogue Practice:** Listen to and practice the dialogue between 민수 and 사라 and 민수 and 사라.
  - `안녕하세요! 처음 뵙겠습니다.` (Hello! Pleased to meet you for the first time.) — *Note: 처음 뵙겠습니다 is the formal introductory greeting to someone new.*
  - `안녕하세요! 만나서 반갑습니다.` (Hello! Nice to meet you.) — *Note: 만나서 반갑습니다 is used by both parties after first greetings.*
  - `오늘 시간 내 주셔서 감사합니다. 안녕히 가세요!` (Thank you for making time today. Goodbye (to the person leaving)!) — *Note: 안녕히 가세요 (Go in peace) is said to the person who is departing.*
  - `네, 민수 씨도 안녕히 계세요!` (Yes, goodbye (stay in peace), Min-su!) — *Note: 안녕히 계세요 (Stay in peace) is said to the person remaining.*
- [ ] **Cultural Etiquette & Pragmatics:** In Korean, bowing at approximately 15 to 30 degrees while saying "안녕하세요" conveys genuine respect. Never shake hands with just one hand to an elder or superior; support your right forearm with your left hand.

#### Module 1.1.2 — 어휘 (Vocabulary in Context)

- [ ] **Core Target Words:** Memorise and pronounce all target words for this lesson:
  - [ ] `안녕하세요` (an-nyeong-ha-se-yo) [Expression]: Hello / Good morning / How are you? — e.g., "선생님, 안녕하세요!" (*Teacher, hello!*)
  - [ ] `만나서 반갑습니다` (man-na-seo ban-gap-seum-ni-da) [Expression]: Nice to meet you — e.g., "처음 뵙겠습니다. 만나서 반갑습니다." (*Pleased to make your acquaintance. Nice to meet you.*)
  - [ ] `안녕히 가세요` (an-nyeong-hi ga-se-yo) [Expression]: Goodbye (said to someone who is leaving) — e.g., "손님, 안녕히 가세요!" (*Customer, goodbye!*)
  - [ ] `안녕히 계세요` (an-nyeong-hi gye-se-yo) [Expression]: Goodbye (said to someone who is staying) — e.g., "저는 먼저 갈게요. 안녕히 계세요!" (*I will head out first. Goodbye (stay well)!*)
  - [ ] `죄송합니다` (joe-song-ham-ni-da, Hanja: 罪悚합니다) [Expression]: I am sorry (polite/formal) — e.g., "늦어서 정말 죄송합니다." (*I am truly sorry for being late.*)
  - [ ] `감사합니다` (gam-sa-ham-ni-da, Hanja: 感謝합니다) [Expression]: Thank you (polite/formal) — e.g., "도와주셔서 감사합니다." (*Thank you for helping me.*)

#### Module 1.1.3 — 문법 (Grammar Formulas & Rules)

- [ ] **Grammar Pattern: `안녕히 가세요 vs 안녕히 계세요`** ("Go peacefully" vs "Stay peacefully")
  - Formula: `가다 (to go) + -세요 → 가세요 // 계시다 (honorific of to stay) + -세요 → 계세요`
  - Explanation: Korean distinguishes whether the person you are parting with is leaving or staying. If both people are leaving the place, both say "안녕히 가세요!"
  - Rule: The speaker leaves, the other person stays: say 안녕히 계세요.
  - Rule: The speaker stays, the other person leaves: say 안녕히 가세요.
  - Rule: Both people leave: both say 안녕히 가세요.
  - Example: `카페를 나갈 때: "안녕히 계세요!"` — *When leaving a cafe: "Stay in peace!"*
  - Example: `집을 나서는 손님에게: "안녕히 가세요!"` — *To a guest leaving your house: "Go in peace!"*

#### Module 1.1.4 — 말하기·듣기 (Shadowing & Drills)

- [ ] **Shadowing & Repetition Drill:** Shadow each native speaker line with authentic bowing intonation.
  - [ ] Prompt: "Say hello respectfully" → Speak aloud: `안녕하세요! 처음 뵙겠습니다.` (*Hello! Pleased to meet you.*)
  - [ ] Prompt: "Say nice to meet you" → Speak aloud: `만나서 반갑습니다.` (*Nice to meet you.*)
  - [ ] Prompt: "Farewell to person staying" → Speak aloud: `안녕히 계세요!` (*Goodbye (stay in peace)!*)
  - [ ] Prompt: "Farewell to person leaving" → Speak aloud: `안녕히 가세요!` (*Goodbye (go in peace)!*)

#### Module 1.1.5 — 읽기·쓰기 (Reading & Production)

- [ ] **Reading Passage:** "First Day Greetings at the Institute"
  - Text: `안녕하세요. 처음 뵙겠습니다. 저는 민수입니다. 만나서 반갑습니다. 안녕히 계세요.`
  - Translation: *Hello. Pleased to meet you for the first time. I am Min-su. Nice to meet you. Goodbye.*
  - [ ] Check: What is the speaker's name? → Answer: `민수 (Min-su)` (*저는 민수입니다.*)
  - [ ] Check: Is the speaker staying or leaving at the end? → Answer: `Leaving` (*He says "안녕히 계세요" to the people staying.*)
- [ ] **Guided Writing Task:** Write a short 3-sentence greeting introducing yourself to a new acquaintance.
  - Sample Model Answer: `안녕하세요! 저는 마이클입니다. 만나서 반갑습니다.`

### 1.2 — Introducing Yourself: Names, Nationalities & Occupations (자기소개: 국적과 직업 말하기)

**Situation & Objective.** First day of Korean language class at Seoul National University.

#### Module 1.2.1 — 대화 (Conversation First)

- [ ] **Dialogue Practice:** Listen to and practice the dialogue between 사라 and 민수 and 사라 and 민수 and 사라.
  - `안녕하세요? 저는 사라예요. 미국 사람이에요.` (Hello? I am Sarah. I am American.) — *Note: 사라 ends in a vowel, so it takes 예요. 사람 ends in a consonant, so it takes 이에요.*
  - `반가워요, 사라 씨! 저는 김민수예요. 한국 사람이에요.` (Nice to meet you, Sarah! I am Kim Min-su. I am Korean.)
  - `민수 씨는 학생이에요?` (Are you a student, Min-su?)
  - `네, 저는 대학생이에요. 사라 씨는 회사원이에요?` (Yes, I am a university student. Are you an office worker, Sarah?)
  - `아니요, 저도 학생이에요.` (No, I am also a student.) — *Note: 도 means "also/too", replacing the topic particle 은/는.*
- [ ] **Cultural Etiquette & Pragmatics:** The title "씨" (e.g. 민수 씨, 사라 씨) is polite and respectful between peers and adult classmates. Never attach "씨" to your own name (e.g., saying "저는 사라 씨예요" is incorrect; say "저는 사라예요").

#### Module 1.2.2 — 어휘 (Vocabulary in Context)

- [ ] **Core Target Words:** Memorise and pronounce all target words for this lesson:
  - [ ] `사람` (sa-ram) [Noun]: Person / people — e.g., "저는 미국 사람이에요." (*I am an American person.*)
  - [ ] `한국` (han-guk, Hanja: 韓國) [Noun]: Korea — e.g., "한국에 여행을 왔어요." (*I came on a trip to Korea.*)
  - [ ] `미국` (mi-guk, Hanja: 美國) [Noun]: United States of America — e.g., "미국 뉴욕에서 살았어요." (*I lived in New York, USA.*)
  - [ ] `학생` (hak-saeng, Hanja: 學生) [Noun]: Student — e.g., "저는 한국어 학생이에요." (*I am a Korean language student.*)
  - [ ] `선생님` (seon-saeng-nim, Hanja: 先生님) [Noun]: Teacher (honorific) — e.g., "선생님, 질문이 있습니다." (*Teacher, I have a question.*)
  - [ ] `회사원` (hoe-sa-won, Hanja: 會社員) [Noun]: Office worker / company employee — e.g., "제 친구는 회사원이에요." (*My friend is an office worker.*)
  - [ ] `의사` (ui-sa, Hanja: 醫師) [Noun]: Doctor / physician — e.g., "병원에 의사 선생님이 계세요." (*The doctor is at the clinic.*)

#### Module 1.2.3 — 문법 (Grammar Formulas & Rules)

- [ ] **Grammar Pattern: `Noun + 이에요 / 예요`** (Am / Is / Are (Polite Identification Copula))
  - Formula: `Noun ending in Consonant + 이에요 // Noun ending in Vowel + 예요`
  - Explanation: The identification copula equates the subject to a noun ("A is B"). In spoken Korean (해요체), the form depends entirely on whether the preceding noun ends with a consonant (받침) or a vowel.
  - Rule: Ends in Consonant (받침 O): 학생 + 이에요 → 학생이에요.
  - Rule: Ends in Vowel (받침 X): 의사 + 예요 → 의사예요.
  - Rule: Question form has the exact same spelling with rising intonation: 학생이에요? (Are you a student?)
  - Example: `저는 학생이에요.` — *I am a student.*
  - Example: `저는 의사예요.` — *I am a doctor.*
  - Example: `한국 사람이에요?` — *Are you Korean?*
- [ ] **Grammar Pattern: `Topic Particle: Noun + 은 / 는`** ("As for N..." / Topic Marker)
  - Formula: `Noun ending in Consonant + 은 // Noun ending in Vowel + 는`
  - Explanation: Marks the topic of the sentence ("As for me...", "Speaking of Min-su..."). It sets the stage or creates a contrast with other topics.
  - Rule: Ends in Consonant: 사람 + 은 → 사람은.
  - Rule: Ends in Vowel: 저 + 는 → 저는.
  - Example: `저는 사라예요.` — *As for me, I am Sarah.*
  - Example: `민수 씨는 학생이에요.` — *As for Min-su, he is a student.*

#### Module 1.2.4 — 말하기·듣기 (Shadowing & Drills)

- [ ] **Shadowing & Repetition Drill:** Substitute nationalities and professions into the pattern "저는 [Country] 사람이에요".
  - [ ] Prompt: "Say: I am American" → Speak aloud: `저는 미국 사람이에요.` (*I am American.*)
  - [ ] Prompt: "Say: I am Japanese" → Speak aloud: `저는 일본 사람이에요.` (*I am Japanese.*)
  - [ ] Prompt: "Say: I am an office worker" → Speak aloud: `저는 회사원이에요.` (*I am an office worker.*)
  - [ ] Prompt: "Ask: Are you a teacher?" → Speak aloud: `선생님이에요?` (*Are you a teacher?*)

#### Module 1.2.5 — 읽기·쓰기 (Reading & Production)

- [ ] **Reading Passage:** "Classmate Self-Introduction Card"
  - Text: `안녕하세요? 제 이름은 타쿠야예요. 저는 일본 사람이에요. 저는 서울대학교 학생이에요. 반갑습니다.`
  - Translation: *Hello? My name is Takuya. I am Japanese. I am a Seoul National University student. Nice to meet you.*
  - [ ] Check: Where is Takuya from? → Answer: `일본 (Japan)` (*저는 일본 사람이에요.*)
  - [ ] Check: What is his occupation? → Answer: `대학생 (University student)` (*서울대학교 학생이에요.*)
- [ ] **Guided Writing Task:** Write your own 4-sentence Korean self-introduction using: greeting, name, nationality, and occupation.
  - Sample Model Answer: `안녕하세요? 저는 안나예요. 저는 독일 사람이에요. 저는 회사원이에요. 반갑습니다!`


## Unit 2 — Unit 2: Classroom Korean & Everyday Objects (제2과: 교실 한국어와 사물)

**Part.** Part A — Learn to Read and Start Speaking (Units 1–3 & Checkpoint A)
**Objective.** Master classroom requests, demonstratives (이거/그거/저거), identifying objects, and negating nouns with 아니에요.

### 2.1 — Classroom Survival: Asking the Teacher for Help (선생님, 질문 있어요!)

**Situation & Objective.** Asking for clarification and instructions during Korean language class.

#### Module 2.1.1 — 대화 (Conversation First)

- [ ] **Dialogue Practice:** Listen to and practice the dialogue between 선생님 and 학생 and 선생님 and 학생.
  - `책 15쪽을 펴세요. 다 읽으셨어요?` (Please open your books to page 15. Have you all read it?)
  - `선생님, 질문 있어요! 잘 모르겠어요.` (Teacher, I have a question! I don't understand well.) — *Note: 질문 있어요 (I have a question) + 잘 모르겠어요 (I don't know/understand well).*
  - `네, 어떤 단어를 모르겠어요?` (Yes, which word do you not understand?)
  - `천천히 다시 말씀해 주세요.` (Please say it again slowly.) — *Note: 천천히 (slowly) + 다시 (again) + 말씀해 주세요 (please speak honorifically).*
- [ ] **Cultural Etiquette & Pragmatics:** When you do not understand something in class, saying "다시 말씀해 주세요" (Please say it again) is polite and expected. Saying just "뭐요?" is considered blunt and rude to an instructor.

#### Module 2.1.2 — 어휘 (Vocabulary in Context)

- [ ] **Core Target Words:** Memorise and pronounce all target words for this lesson:
  - [ ] `질문` (jil-mun, Hanja: 質問) [Noun]: Question — e.g., "질문이 있으면 손을 드세요." (*If you have a question, please raise your hand.*)
  - [ ] `책` (chaek, Hanja: 冊) [Noun]: Book — e.g., "책을 펴세요." (*Please open your book.*)
  - [ ] `천천히` (cheon-cheon-hi) [Adverb]: Slowly — e.g., "천천히 읽어 주세요." (*Please read slowly.*)
  - [ ] `다시` (da-si) [Adverb]: Again / once more — e.g., "다시 한번 들어 보세요." (*Please listen once more.*)
  - [ ] `공책` (gong-chaek, Hanja: 空冊) [Noun]: Notebook — e.g., "공책에 필기하세요." (*Please take notes in your notebook.*)

#### Module 2.1.3 — 문법 (Grammar Formulas & Rules)

- [ ] **Grammar Pattern: `Verb Stem + -(으)세요 (Polite Request)`** (Please do [Action] / Imperative Request)
  - Formula: `Consonant stem + 으세요 // Vowel stem + 세요`
  - Explanation: Used by teachers and elders to give polite directions or instructions. Common classroom verbs: 읽다 → 읽으세요 (please read), 보다 → 보세요 (please look).
  - Rule: 닫다 (to close) → 닫으세요.
  - Rule: 쓰다 (to write) → 쓰세요.
  - Rule: 듣다 (to listen - ㄷ irregular) → 들으세요.
  - Example: `책을 보세요.` — *Please look at the book.*
  - Example: `따라 읽으세요.` — *Please read along.*

#### Module 2.1.4 — 말하기·듣기 (Shadowing & Drills)

- [ ] **Shadowing & Repetition Drill:** Practice requesting help from the teacher clearly.
  - [ ] Prompt: "Request: Please speak slowly" → Speak aloud: `천천히 말씀해 주세요.` (*Please speak slowly.*)
  - [ ] Prompt: "Request: Please say it once more" → Speak aloud: `다시 한번 말씀해 주세요.` (*Please say it once again.*)
  - [ ] Prompt: "Statement: I have a question" → Speak aloud: `선생님, 질문 있어요!` (*Teacher, I have a question!*)

#### Module 2.1.5 — 읽기·쓰기 (Reading & Production)

- [ ] **Reading Passage:** "Classroom Instruction Board"
  - Text: `여러분, 교재를 펴세요. 오디오를 들으세요. 그리고 공책에 정답을 쓰세요. 질문이 있으면 손을 드세요.`
  - Translation: *Everyone, please open your textbooks. Listen to the audio. And write the correct answers in your notebook. If you have questions, please raise your hand.*
  - [ ] Check: Where should students write answers? → Answer: `공책에 (in notebook)` (*공책에 정답을 쓰세요.*)
  - [ ] Check: What should you do if you have a question? → Answer: `손을 들다 (raise hand)` (*손을 드세요.*)
- [ ] **Guided Writing Task:** Write 3 polite requests you would say to your Korean teacher in class.
  - Sample Model Answer: `선생님, 질문 있어요. 천천히 말씀해 주세요. 감사합니다.`

### 2.2 — Asking What an Object Is: Demonstratives & Negation (이거, 그거, 저거와 사물 이름)

**Situation & Objective.** Asking about unfamiliar items in a stationery shop or on a desk.

#### Module 2.2.1 — 대화 (Conversation First)

- [ ] **Dialogue Practice:** Listen to and practice the dialogue between 지우 and 민호 and 지우 and 민호.
  - `민호 씨, 저게 뭐예요?` (Min-ho, what is that over there?) — *Note: 저것이 contracted to 저게 in natural speech.*
  - `저거요? 저거는 전자사전이에요.` (That over there? That is an electronic dictionary.)
  - `아, 스마트폰이 아니에요?` (Ah, is it not a smartphone?) — *Note: 아니에요 is the negative copula ("is not").*
  - `네, 스마트폰이 아니에요. 사전이에요.` (Yes, it is not a smartphone. It is a dictionary.)
- [ ] **Cultural Etiquette & Pragmatics:** 이거 (this near speaker), 그거 (that near listener), 저거 (that over there far from both). When spoken casually, 이것/그것/저것 contracted to 이거/그거/저거.

#### Module 2.2.2 — 어휘 (Vocabulary in Context)

- [ ] **Core Target Words:** Memorise and pronounce all target words for this lesson:
  - [ ] `이거` (i-geo) [Noun]: This item (near speaker) — e.g., "이거 얼마예요?" (*How much is this?*)
  - [ ] `그거` (geu-geo) [Noun]: That item (near listener) — e.g., "그거 보여 주세요." (*Please show me that.*)
  - [ ] `저거` (jeo-geo) [Noun]: That item over there (far from both) — e.g., "저거 참 예쁘네요." (*That over there is really pretty.*)
  - [ ] `시계` (si-gye, Hanja: 時計) [Noun]: Clock / watch — e.g., "벽에 시계가 있어요." (*There is a clock on the wall.*)
  - [ ] `가방` (ga-bang) [Noun]: Bag / backpack — e.g., "가방이 아주 무거워요." (*The bag is very heavy.*)
  - [ ] `지갑` (ji-gap, Hanja: 紙匣) [Noun]: Wallet / purse — e.g., "지갑에 현금이 없어요." (*There is no cash in my wallet.*)

#### Module 2.2.3 — 문법 (Grammar Formulas & Rules)

- [ ] **Grammar Pattern: `Demonstratives: 이거 / 그거 / 저거`** (This / That (near you) / That (over there))
  - Formula: `이거 (near speaker) // 그거 (near listener) // 저거 (distant from both)`
  - Explanation: Refers to physical objects based on proximity to the speaker and listener. When attached to nouns as modifiers, use 이 [Noun], 그 [Noun], 저 [Noun] (e.g., 이 책, 그 가방, 저 건물).
  - Rule: 이것이 → 이게 (Subject).
  - Rule: 이것은 → 이건 (Topic).
  - Rule: 이것을 → 이걸 (Object).
  - Example: `이게 뭐예요? — 시계예요.` — *What is this? — It is a watch.*
  - Example: `그건 제 가방이에요.` — *That is my bag.*
  - Example: `저 시계가 비싸요.` — *That watch over there is expensive.*
- [ ] **Grammar Pattern: `Noun Negation: Noun + 이/가 아니에요`** (Am not / Is not / Are not Noun)
  - Formula: `Consonant noun + 이 아니에요 // Vowel noun + 가 아니에요`
  - Explanation: The negative counterpart of 이에요/예요. Always requires the subject particle 이 or 가 attached to the preceding noun.
  - Rule: 학생 (consonant) + 이 아니에요 → 학생이 아니에요 (I am not a student).
  - Rule: 의사 (vowel) + 가 아니에요 → 의사가 아니에요 (I am not a doctor).
  - Example: `저는 일본 사람이 아니에요.` — *I am not Japanese.*
  - Example: `이건 제 지갑이 아니에요.` — *This is not my wallet.*

#### Module 2.2.4 — 말하기·듣기 (Shadowing & Drills)

- [ ] **Shadowing & Repetition Drill:** Ask what an object is and deny incorrect guesses.
  - [ ] Prompt: "Ask: What is this?" → Speak aloud: `이게 뭐예요?` (*What is this?*)
  - [ ] Prompt: "Answer: It is a wallet" → Speak aloud: `지갑이에요.` (*It is a wallet.*)
  - [ ] Prompt: "Deny: It is not a smartphone" → Speak aloud: `스마트폰이 아니에요.` (*It is not a smartphone.*)

#### Module 2.2.5 — 읽기·쓰기 (Reading & Production)

- [ ] **Reading Passage:** "Desk Inspection Note"
  - Text: `책상 위에 물건이 많아요. 이건 한국어 책이에요. 공책이 아니에요. 저건 제 스마트폰이에요. 지갑은 가방 안에 있어요.`
  - Translation: *There are many items on the desk. This is a Korean book. It is not a notebook. That over there is my smartphone. The wallet is inside the bag.*
  - [ ] Check: Is the item on the desk a notebook? → Answer: `No` (*공책이 아니에요 (It is not a notebook).*)
  - [ ] Check: Where is the wallet? → Answer: `가방 안에 (inside the bag)` (*가방 안에 있어요.*)
- [ ] **Guided Writing Task:** Write 3 sentences identifying 3 objects on your desk and negating what they are not.
  - Sample Model Answer: `이건 제 노트북이에요. 태블릿이 아니에요. 저건 제 텀블러예요.`


## Unit 3 — Unit 3: People & Belongings (제3과: 사람과 물건 (소유))

**Part.** Part A — Learn to Read and Start Speaking (Units 1–3 & Checkpoint A)
**Objective.** Learn possessive particle 의, existence verb 있어요/없어요, and describing what someone has.

### 3.1 — Asking About a Friend's Bag: Possession (의) (이 가방 누구 거예요?)

**Situation & Objective.** Finding a lost bag in a study lounge and locating the owner.

#### Module 3.1.1 — 대화 (Conversation First)

- [ ] **Dialogue Practice:** Listen to and practice the dialogue between 유진 and 민수 and 유진 and 민수.
  - `민수 씨, 이 검은색 가방 민수 씨 거예요?` (Min-su, is this black bag yours?) — *Note: 민수 씨 거예요? = Is it Min-su's?*
  - `아니요, 제 가방이 아니에요. 제 가방은 파란색이에요.` (No, it is not my bag. My bag is blue.) — *Note: 저의 (my) contracts to 제. 나 + 의 contracts to 내.*
  - `그럼 이 가방은 누구 거예요?` (Then whose bag is this?)
  - `마이클 씨의 가방이에요. 아까 찾고 있었어요.` (It is Michael's bag. He was looking for it earlier.)
- [ ] **Cultural Etiquette & Pragmatics:** The possessive particle "의" is pronounced [에] in spoken conversational Korean (e.g. 민수의 is pronounced [민수에]). In everyday speech, "의 것" (thing of...) contracts to "거" (누구 거예요? = Whose is it?).

#### Module 3.1.2 — 어휘 (Vocabulary in Context)

- [ ] **Core Target Words:** Memorise and pronounce all target words for this lesson:
  - [ ] `누구` (nu-gu) [Noun]: Who / whose — e.g., "저 사람은 누구예요?" (*Who is that person over there?*)
  - [ ] `제` (je) [Noun]: My (polite, contracted from 저의) — e.g., "이건 제 책이에요." (*This is my book.*)
  - [ ] `내` (nae) [Noun]: My (casual, contracted from 나의) — e.g., "내 우산 어디 있어?" (*Where is my umbrella?*)
  - [ ] `우산` (u-san, Hanja: 雨傘) [Noun]: Umbrella — e.g., "비가 오니까 우산을 챙기세요." (*Since it is raining, take an umbrella.*)
  - [ ] `모자` (mo-ja, Hanja: 帽子) [Noun]: Hat / cap — e.g., "이 모자 정말 멋져요." (*This hat is really cool.*)

#### Module 3.1.3 — 문법 (Grammar Formulas & Rules)

- [ ] **Grammar Pattern: `Possessive Particle: Noun + 의`** ("Of" / Possessive 's)
  - Formula: `Noun + 의 (pronounced [에] in speech)`
  - Explanation: Attaches to a noun to show ownership. Common contractions: 저의 → 제 (my, polite), 나의 → 내 (my, casual), 너의 → 네 / 네 [pronounced 니] (your).
  - Rule: 선생님의 책 [선생니메 책] = Teacher's book.
  - Rule: 누구의 것 → 누구 거 = Whose item.
  - Rule: 제 우산 = My umbrella.
  - Example: `이건 제 친구의 가방이에요.` — *This is my friend's bag.*
  - Example: `누구 거역시요? — 제 거예요.` — *Whose is it? — It is mine.*

#### Module 3.1.4 — 말하기·듣기 (Shadowing & Drills)

- [ ] **Shadowing & Repetition Drill:** Practice claiming and identifying who items belong to.
  - [ ] Prompt: "Ask: Whose watch is this?" → Speak aloud: `이 시계 누구 거예요?` (*Whose watch is this?*)
  - [ ] Prompt: "Answer: It is my watch" → Speak aloud: `제 시계예요.` (*It is my watch.*)
  - [ ] Prompt: "State: It is Min-su's umbrella" → Speak aloud: `민수 씨 우산이에요.` (*It is Min-su's umbrella.*)

#### Module 3.1.5 — 읽기·쓰기 (Reading & Production)

- [ ] **Reading Passage:** "Lost & Found Notice"
  - Text: `휴게실에 검은색 가방이 있습니다. 이 가방은 선생님의 가방이 아닙니다. 마이클 씨의 가방입니다. 3층 교무실로 오세요.`
  - Translation: *There is a black bag in the lounge. This bag is not the teacher's bag. It is Michael's bag. Please come to the 3rd floor teachers' office.*
  - [ ] Check: Whose bag is it? → Answer: `마이클 씨 (Michael)` (*마이클 씨의 가방입니다.*)
  - [ ] Check: Where should he pick it up? → Answer: `3층 교무실 (3rd floor teachers' office)` (*3층 교무실로 오세요.*)
- [ ] **Guided Writing Task:** Write 3 sentences describing 3 items you have and who they belong to.
  - Sample Model Answer: `이건 제 스마트폰이에요. 저건 제 룸메이트의 우산이에요. 이 열쇠는 누구 거예요?`

### 3.2 — Asking What Someone Has: 있어요 and 없어요 (펜 있어요? — 있어요와 없어요)

**Situation & Objective.** Borrowing stationery and asking if a classmate has something.

#### Module 3.2.1 — 대화 (Conversation First)

- [ ] **Dialogue Practice:** Listen to and practice the dialogue between 지민 and 유진 and 지민 and 유진.
  - `유진 씨, 혹시 파란색 펜 있어요?` (Yu-jin, by any chance do you have a blue pen?) — *Note: 혹시 (by any chance) + 펜 있어요? (Do you have a pen?)*
  - `네, 필통에 펜 있어요. 여기 있어요.` (Yes, I have a pen in my pencil case. Here it is.)
  - `지우개도 있어요?` (Do you have an eraser too?)
  - `아니요, 지우개는 없어요. 연필만 있어요.` (No, I don't have an eraser. I only have a pencil.) — *Note: 만 means "only", attached directly to the noun.*
- [ ] **Cultural Etiquette & Pragmatics:** 있어요 means both "to exist / be located" AND "to possess / have".없어요 is its single negative counterpart ("does not exist" or "do not have"). Korean does not use "안 있어요".

#### Module 3.2.2 — 어휘 (Vocabulary in Context)

- [ ] **Core Target Words:** Memorise and pronounce all target words for this lesson:
  - [ ] `있다` (it-da) [Verb]: To have / to exist (polite: 있어요) — e.g., "저는 지금 시간이 있어요." (*I have time right now.*)
  - [ ] `없다` (eop-da) [Verb]: To not have / to not exist (polite: 없어요) — e.g., "지갑에 돈이 없어요." (*I have no money in my wallet.*)
  - [ ] `펜` (pen) [Noun]: Pen — e.g., "펜으로 이름을 쓰세요." (*Please write your name with a pen.*)
  - [ ] `지우개` (ji-u-gae) [Noun]: Eraser — e.g., "지우개로 글씨를 지웠어요." (*I erased the writing with an eraser.*)
  - [ ] `돈` (don) [Noun]: Money — e.g., "교통카드를 살 돈이 있어요." (*I have money to buy a transit card.*)

#### Module 3.2.3 — 문법 (Grammar Formulas & Rules)

- [ ] **Grammar Pattern: `Noun + 이/가 있어요 / 없어요 (Possession)`** (To have / To not have Noun)
  - Formula: `Consonant noun + 이 있어요 // Vowel noun + 가 있어요`
  - Explanation: Expresses possession. Notice that Korean puts the subject particle 이/가 on the item you have, unlike English where the item is a direct object.
  - Rule: 시간이 있어요 = I have time.
  - Rule: 친구가 없어요 = I don't have friends.
  - Rule: Never say "안 있어요" — the opposite of 있어요 is always 없어요.
  - Example: `우산이 있어요?` — *Do you have an umbrella?*
  - Example: `네, 있어요. / 아니요, 없어요.` — *Yes, I have it. / No, I don't.*

#### Module 3.2.4 — 말하기·듣기 (Shadowing & Drills)

- [ ] **Shadowing & Repetition Drill:** Ask and answer about items in your bag using 있어요 / 없어요.
  - [ ] Prompt: "Ask: Do you have money?" → Speak aloud: `돈이 있어요?` (*Do you have money?*)
  - [ ] Prompt: "Answer: No, I don't have money" → Speak aloud: `아니요, 돈이 없어요.` (*No, I don't have money.*)
  - [ ] Prompt: "Ask: Do you have time today?" → Speak aloud: `오늘 시간 있어요?` (*Do you have time today?*)

#### Module 3.2.5 — 읽기·쓰기 (Reading & Production)

- [ ] **Reading Passage:** "What is Inside Sarah's Backpack?"
  - Text: `제 가방 안에 책과 공책이 있어요. 필통도 있어요. 필통 안에 볼펜이 있어요. 하지만 지우개는 없어요. 스마트폰도 가방에 있어요.`
  - Translation: *Inside my bag there are books and notebooks. There is also a pencil case. Inside the pencil case there is a ballpoint pen. However, there is no eraser. My smartphone is also in the bag.*
  - [ ] Check: What is inside the pencil case? → Answer: `볼펜 (ballpoint pen)` (*필통 안에 볼펜이 있어요.*)
  - [ ] Check: Does Sarah have an eraser? → Answer: `No` (*지우개는 없어요.*)
- [ ] **Guided Writing Task:** Write 4 sentences describing what you have and do not have in your bag right now.
  - Sample Model Answer: `제 가방에 지갑이 있어요. 이어폰도 있어요. 하지만 우산은 없어요. 현금도 없어요.`


## Checkpoint A — Checkpoint A: Foundations of Reading & Self-Introduction

**Prerequisites.** Completion of Unit 0, Unit 1, Unit 2, and Unit 3.
**Target Skills:**
- [ ] Read and write any basic Hangul word aloud without relying on romanization.
- [ ] Introduce yourself (name, nationality, occupation) politely using 이에요/예요 and 은/는.
- [ ] Ask what objects are and clarify using demonstratives (이거, 그거, 저거) and 아니에요.
- [ ] State what items you have or do not have using 있어요 / 없어요.

### Spoken Simulation
- **Scenario:** You meet a new student at the international student lounge at Seoul National University.
- **Prompt:** Greet them, introduce your name, state where you are from, ask what their item is, and confirm whether you have a pen.
- [ ] Deliver unprompted: `안녕하세요! 처음 뵙겠습니다. 저는 사라예요. 미국 사람이에요. 그리고 대학생이에요. 이게 민수 씨 책이에요? 저도 펜이 있어요. 만나서 반갑습니다!` (*Hello! Pleased to meet you. I am Sarah. I am American. And I am a university student. Is this Min-su's book? I also have a pen. Nice to meet you!*)

### Written Milestone Assignment
- **Prompt:** Write a 6-sentence introductory profile for your student club application. (40–60 Korean words)
- [ ] Submit written draft: `안녕하세요? 제 이름은 데이비드예요. 저는 캐나다 사람이에요. 저는 서울대학교 어학당 학생이에요. 한국어를 열심히 공부해요. 제 가방에 한국어 책과 사전이 있어요. 반갑습니다!`

## Unit 4 — Places and Locations (장소와 위치)

**Part.** Part B — Handle Everyday Needs (Units 4–8 & Checkpoint B)
**Objective.** Explain where objects or places are located using location particles, existence verbs, and positional prepositions (앞, 뒤, 옆, 안, 밖, 위, 밑).

### 4.1 — Finding an Object in a Room (방에서 물건 찾기)

**Situation & Objective.** Describe the location of household and classroom objects using location marker 에 and 있어요/없어요.

#### Module 4.1.1 — 대화 (Conversation First)

- [ ] **Dialogue Practice:** Listen to and practice the dialogue between Sarah (사라) and Minsu (민수) and Sarah (사라) and Minsu (민수) and Sarah (사라).
  - `민수 씨, 제 한국어 책이 어디에 있어요?` (Min-su, where is my Korean book?) — *Note: 어디에 = "at where" (어디 + 에 location marker).*
  - `책상 위에 있어요. 안경도 책상 위에 있어요.` (It is on the desk. The glasses are also on the desk.) — *Note: 책상 위 = desk top; 위 = above/on.*
  - `아, 찾았어요! 그러면 제 가방은 어디에 있어요?` (Ah, I found it! Then where is my bag?) — *Note: 그러면 = "then / in that case".*
  - `가방은 의자 밑에 있어요. 침대 옆이 아니에요.` (The bag is under the chair. It is not next to the bed.) — *Note: 밑 = under/below; 침대 옆 = next to the bed.*
  - `정말 고마워요, 민수 씨!` (Thank you so much, Min-su!) — *Note: Friendly polite expression of thanks.*
- [ ] **Cultural Etiquette & Pragmatics:** In Korean homes and student dormitories (기숙사), asking where shared or personal study materials are located is everyday conversation. When indicating positions, Korean places the reference noun before the position noun: [Noun] + [위/밑/앞/뒤/옆] + 에 있어요 (e.g. 책상 위에 있어요, literally "desk top-at is").

#### Module 4.1.2 — 어휘 (Vocabulary in Context)

- [ ] **Core Target Words:** Memorise and pronounce all target words for this lesson:
  - [ ] `방` (bang, Hanja: 房) [Noun]: room — e.g., "제 방이 아주 따뜻해요." (*My room is very warm.*)
  - [ ] `책상` (chaeksang, Hanja: 冊床) [Noun]: desk — e.g., "책상 위에 컴퓨터가 있어요." (*There is a computer on the desk.*)
  - [ ] `의자` (uija, Hanja: 椅子) [Noun]: chair — e.g., "의자에 앉으세요." (*Please sit on the chair.*)
  - [ ] `침대` (chimdae, Hanja: 寢臺) [Noun]: bed — e.g., "침대 밑에 슬리퍼가 있어요." (*There are slippers under the bed.*)
  - [ ] `위` (wi) [Noun]: top / above / on — e.g., "탁자 위에 커피가 있어요." (*There is coffee on the table.*)
  - [ ] `밑 / 아래` (mit / arae) [Noun]: bottom / below / underneath — e.g., "의자 밑에 고양이가 있어요." (*There is a cat under the chair.*)
  - [ ] `어디` (eodi) [Noun]: where (interrogative pronoun) — e.g., "화장실이 어디예요?" (*Where is the restroom?*)

#### Module 4.1.3 — 문법 (Grammar Formulas & Rules)

- [ ] **Grammar Pattern: `Noun + 에 있어요/없어요`** (to exist / not exist in/at a place (Location marker 에))
  - Formula: `[Noun (Location)] + 에 + [Subject]이/가 + 있어요/없어요`
  - Explanation: The particle -에 marks static location where someone or something exists, sits, or remains. Contrast this with action location marker -에서 which marks where an action takes place.
  - Rule: Attach -에 directly to the place noun, regardless of whether it ends in a vowel or consonant: 방 + 에 -> 방에, 학교 + 에 -> 학교에.
  - Rule: Use 있어요 to state that the subject is present at that location.
  - Rule: Use 없어요 to state that the subject is absent from that location.
  - Contrast Note: Do not confuse location "에" (static existence with 있어요/없어요, 살아요, 있어요) with destination "에" (direction with 가요/와요).
  - Example: `선생님이 교실에 있어요.` — *The teacher is in the classroom.* [선생님 (teacher) + 이 (subject) + 교실 (classroom) + 에 (at) + 있어요 (is)]
  - Example: `민수 씨는 지금 집에 없어요.` — *Minsu is not at home right now.* [민수 씨는 (As for Minsu) + 지금 (now) + 집 (home) + 에 (at) + 없어요 (is not)]
- [ ] **Grammar Pattern: `Noun + [위/밑/앞/뒤/옆/안/밖] + 에`** (on / under / in front of / behind / beside / inside / outside)
  - Formula: `[Reference Noun] + [Position Word] + 에`
  - Explanation: In Korean, positional words like 위 (top), 밑 (under), 앞 (front), 뒤 (back), 옆 (side), 안 (inside), and 밖 (outside) act as spatial nouns. You place the reference object first, followed by the spatial noun, followed by the location particle 에.
  - Rule: 책상 위 (desk top) + 에 = 책상 위에 (on the desk)
  - Rule: 의자 밑 (chair bottom) + 에 = 의자 밑에 (under the chair)
  - Rule: 가방 안 (bag inside) + 에 = 가방 안에 (inside the bag)
  - Example: `스마트폰이 가방 안에 있어요.` — *The smartphone is inside the bag.* [스마트폰 + 이 (subject) + 가방 (bag) + 안 (inside) + 에 (at) + 있어요]
  - Example: `고양이가 침대 밑에 있어요.` — *The cat is underneath the bed.* [고양이 (cat) + 가 (subject) + 침대 (bed) + 밑 (under) + 에 (at) + 있어요]

#### Module 4.1.4 — 말하기·듣기 (Shadowing & Drills)

- [ ] **Shadowing & Repetition Drill:** Listen carefully, repeat aloud, and substitute the nouns to describe where things are located.
  - [ ] Prompt: "Where is the book? (On the desk)" → Speak aloud: `책이 책상 위에 있어요.` (*The book is on the desk.*)
  - [ ] Prompt: "Where is the wallet? (Under the chair)" → Speak aloud: `지갑이 의자 밑에 있어요.` (*The wallet is under the chair.*)
  - [ ] Prompt: "Where is the pencil? (Inside the pencil case)" → Speak aloud: `연필이 필통 안에 있어요.` (*The pencil is inside the pencil case.*)
  - [ ] Prompt: "Is Min-su at home right now?" → Speak aloud: `아니요, 지금 집에 없어요.` (*No, he is not at home right now.*)

#### Module 4.1.5 — 읽기·쓰기 (Reading & Production)

- [ ] **Reading Passage:** "사라의 방 (Sarah's Room)"
  - Text: `여기는 제 방이에요. 제 방에 큰 책상이 있어요. 책상 위에 노트북과 한국어 사전이 있어요. 책상 옆에 편안한 의자가 있어요. 침대 위에는 베개와 이불이 있어요. 하지만 방에 텔레비전은 없어요.`
  - Translation: *This is my room. In my room, there is a big desk. On the desk, there is a laptop and a Korean dictionary. Beside the desk, there is a comfortable chair. On the bed, there are pillows and a blanket. However, there is no television in the room.*
  - [ ] Check: What is on Sarah's desk? → Answer: `노트북과 한국어 사전 (Laptop and Korean dictionary)` (*책상 위에 노트북과 한국어 사전이 있어요.*)
  - [ ] Check: Is there a television in Sarah's room? → Answer: `No` (*하지만 방에 텔레비전은 없어요.*)
- [ ] **Guided Writing Task:** Describe your study room or bedroom in 5 Korean sentences using location words (위, 밑, 옆, 안) and 있어요/없어요.
  - Sample Model Answer: `여기는 제 방이에요. 방에 책상이 있어요. 책상 위에 컴퓨터가 있어요. 책상 옆에 침대가 있어요. 하지만 방에 텔레비전은 없어요.`

### 4.2 — Asking Where a Place or Building Is (카페가 어디예요?)

**Situation & Objective.** Inquire about and give directions to facilities, stores, and buildings using 앞, 뒤, 옆, and 건너편.

#### Module 4.2.1 — 대화 (Conversation First)

- [ ] **Dialogue Practice:** Listen to and practice the dialogue between David (데이비드) and Passerby (행인) and David (데이비드) and Passerby (행인) and David (데이비드).
  - `실례합니다, 여기 스타벅스 카페가 어디예요?` (Excuse me, where is the Starbucks cafe here?) — *Note: 실례합니다 = "Excuse me" (standard polite opener when asking a stranger).*
  - `저기 지하철역 출구 앞을 보세요. 은행이 있어요.` (Look in front of the subway station exit over there. There is a bank.) — *Note: 출구 = exit; 은행 = bank.*
  - `네, 국민은행이 보여요.` (Yes, I see the KB Kookmin Bank.) — *Note: 보여요 = "is visible / I see it".*
  - `카페는 은행 바로 옆에 있어요. 약국 뒤예요.` (The cafe is right next to the bank. It is behind the pharmacy.) — *Note: 바로 = "right / immediately"; 약국 = pharmacy.*
  - `아, 찾았습니다. 정말 감사합니다!` (Ah, I found it. Thank you very much!) — *Note: Formal polite acknowledgment and gratitude.*
- [ ] **Cultural Etiquette & Pragmatics:** When asking strangers on the street for directions in Seoul, start with "실례합니다" (Excuse me). Koreans frequently use visible landmarks such as subway station exits (지하철역 출구), convenience stores (편의점), and banks (은행) as navigational anchors.

#### Module 4.2.2 — 어휘 (Vocabulary in Context)

- [ ] **Core Target Words:** Memorise and pronounce all target words for this lesson:
  - [ ] `카페` (kape) [Noun]: cafe / coffee shop — e.g., "카페에서 친구를 만나요." (*I meet a friend at the cafe.*)
  - [ ] `지하철역` (jihacheol-yeok, Hanja: 地下鐵驛) [Noun]: subway station — e.g., "강남역 2번 출구로 오세요." (*Please come to Gangnam Station Exit 2.*)
  - [ ] `은행` (eunhaeng, Hanja: 銀行) [Noun]: bank — e.g., "은행이 우체국 옆에 있어요." (*The bank is next to the post office.*)
  - [ ] `약국` (yakguk, Hanja: 藥局) [Noun]: pharmacy — e.g., "약국에서 감기약을 사요." (*I buy cold medicine at the pharmacy.*)
  - [ ] `앞` (ap) [Noun]: front — e.g., "학교 앞에서 만나요." (*Let us meet in front of the school.*)
  - [ ] `뒤` (dwi) [Noun]: back / behind — e.g., "건물 뒤에 주차장이 있어요." (*There is a parking lot behind the building.*)
  - [ ] `옆` (yeop) [Noun]: side / next to — e.g., "제 옆에 앉으세요." (*Please sit next to me.*)
  - [ ] `건너편 / 맞은편` (geonneopyeon / majeunpyeon) [Noun]: across the street / opposite side — e.g., "서점이 길 건너편에 있어요." (*The bookstore is across the street.*)

#### Module 4.2.3 — 문법 (Grammar Formulas & Rules)

- [ ] **Grammar Pattern: `Noun + 이/가 어디예요? / 어디에 있어요?`** (Where is [Noun]?)
  - Formula: `[Noun] + 이/가 어디예요? OR [Noun] + 은/는 어디에 있어요?`
  - Explanation: Both forms ask for location. "어디예요?" asks what/where the location identity is, while "어디에 있어요?" asks at what physical point the subject is located. Both are completely natural.
  - Rule: Noun ends in consonant: 화장실이 어디예요? / 화장실은 어디에 있어요?
  - Rule: Noun ends in vowel: 카페가 어디예요? / 카페는 어디에 있어요?
  - Example: `편의점이 어디예요?` — *Where is the convenience store?* [편의점 (convenience store) + 이 (subject) + 어디예요? (where is it?)]
  - Example: `지하철역은 어디에 있어요?` — *As for the subway station, where is it located?* [지하철역 (subway station) + 은 (topic) + 어디 (where) + 에 (at) + 있어요? (is it?)]
- [ ] **Grammar Pattern: `Noun 1 + [앞/뒤/옆/사이/건너편] + 에 있어요`** (It is in front of / behind / next to / between / across from Noun 1)
  - Formula: `[Reference Building] + [Spatial Relation] + 에 있어요`
  - Explanation: Provides exact spatial orientation relative to a well-known building or landmark.
  - Rule: 건물 앞 = in front of the building -> 건물 앞에 있어요
  - Rule: 식당 뒤 = behind the restaurant -> 식당 뒤에 있어요
  - Rule: 은행과 우체국 사이 = between the bank and post office -> 은행과 우체국 사이에 있어요
  - Example: `약국이 병원 바로 옆에 있어요.` — *The pharmacy is right next to the hospital.* [약국 (pharmacy) + 이 (subject) + 병원 (hospital) + 바로 (right) + 옆 (side) + 에 있어요]
  - Example: `버스 정류장은 백화점 맞은편에 있어요.` — *The bus stop is across from the department store.* [버스 정류장 (bus stop) + 은 (topic) + 백화점 (dept store) + 맞은편 (opposite side) + 에 있어요]

#### Module 4.2.4 — 말하기·듣기 (Shadowing & Drills)

- [ ] **Shadowing & Repetition Drill:** Practice asking for everyday city landmarks and answering using spatial prepositions.
  - [ ] Prompt: "Ask where the subway station is." → Speak aloud: `실례합니다, 지하철역이 어디예요?` (*Excuse me, where is the subway station?*)
  - [ ] Prompt: "Answer: It is behind the school." → Speak aloud: `학교 뒤에 있어요.` (*It is behind the school.*)
  - [ ] Prompt: "Ask where the convenience store is." → Speak aloud: `편의점이 어디에 있어요?` (*Where is the convenience store?*)
  - [ ] Prompt: "Answer: It is right next to the bank." → Speak aloud: `은행 바로 옆에 있어요.` (*It is right next to the bank.*)

#### Module 4.2.5 — 읽기·쓰기 (Reading & Production)

- [ ] **Reading Passage:** "우리 동네 지도 (Our Neighborhood Map)"
  - Text: `우리 동네는 아주 살기 좋아요. 지하철역 1번 출구 앞에 편의점이 있어요. 편의점 건너편에 맛있는 빵집이 있어요. 빵집 옆에는 큰 병원이 있어요. 그리고 병원 뒤에 조용한 도서관이 있어요.`
  - Translation: *Our neighborhood is very pleasant to live in. There is a convenience store in front of subway station Exit 1. Across from the convenience store, there is a delicious bakery. Next to the bakery, there is a large hospital. And behind the hospital, there is a quiet library.*
  - [ ] Check: What is located across from the convenience store? → Answer: `빵집 (bakery)` (*편의점 건너편에 맛있는 빵집이 있어요.*)
  - [ ] Check: Where is the library located? → Answer: `병원 뒤 (behind the hospital)` (*병원 뒤에 조용한 도서관이 있어요.*)
- [ ] **Guided Writing Task:** Write 5-6 sentences describing the buildings and landmarks around your home or school campus.
  - Sample Model Answer: `제 학교 근처에 많은 건물이 있어요. 정문 앞에 지하철역이 있어요. 지하철역 옆에 편의점이 있어요. 편의점 건너편에는 서점이 있어요. 학교 뒤에 아름다운 공원도 있어요.`


## Unit 5 — Food and Ordering (음식과 주문하기)

**Part.** Part B — Handle Everyday Needs (Units 4–8 & Checkpoint B)
**Objective.** Order drinks and meals confidently, specify quantities with native Korean numbers and counters (개, 잔, 병), and link items using 하고 and 와/과.

### 5.1 — Ordering a Drink at a Café (카페에서 음료 주문하기)

**Situation & Objective.** Order drinks, request quantities with native numbers (하나, 둘, 셋) and counter 잔, and use 주세요 politely.

#### Module 5.1.1 — 대화 (Conversation First)

- [ ] **Dialogue Practice:** Listen to and practice the dialogue between Barista (바리스타) and Sarah (사라) and Barista (바리스타) and Sarah (사라) and Barista (바리스타).
  - `어서 오세요! 주문하시겠어요?` (Welcome! Would you like to order?) — *Note: 주문하시겠어요? = Standard honorific polite greeting at food establishments.*
  - `네, 따뜻한 아메리카노 한 잔 주세요.` (Yes, please give me one warm Americano.) — *Note: 따뜻한 = warm/hot; 한 잔 = one cup.*
  - `더 필요하신 것은 없으세요?` (Is there anything else you need?) — *Note: Standard polite question checking for additional items.*
  - `그리고 치즈 케이크 하나 주세요. 포장해 주세요.` (And please give me one cheese cake. Please wrap it to go.) — *Note: 포장해 주세요 = "Please package it / to go".*
  - `네, 알겠습니다. 진동벨을 받으세요.` (Yes, understood. Please take the buzzer pager.) — *Note: 진동벨 = vibrating buzzer pager ubiquitous in Korean cafes.*
- [ ] **Cultural Etiquette & Pragmatics:** In Korean cafes, the default iced drink is often abbreviated: "아아" (아이스 아메리카노). When ordering, state the drink temperature first (따뜻한 for hot, 아이스 for iced), followed by the item name, the quantity with counter (한 잔, 두 잔), and finish with "주세요" (please give me). If you want takeaway, say "포장해 주세요" or "테이크아웃해 주세요".

#### Module 5.1.2 — 어휘 (Vocabulary in Context)

- [ ] **Core Target Words:** Memorise and pronounce all target words for this lesson:
  - [ ] `커피` (keopi) [Noun]: coffee — e.g., "저는 아침마다 커피를 마셔요." (*I drink coffee every morning.*)
  - [ ] `녹차` (nokcha, Hanja: 綠茶) [Noun]: green tea — e.g., "따뜻한 녹차 두 잔 주세요." (*Please give me two cups of hot green tea.*)
  - [ ] `물` (mul) [Noun]: water — e.g., "시원한 물 한 잔 주세요." (*Please give me a glass of cold water.*)
  - [ ] `주스` (juseu) [Noun]: juice — e.g., "오렌지 주스 세 잔 있어요." (*There are three glasses of orange juice.*)
  - [ ] `잔` (jan, Hanja: 盞) [Counter]: counter for cups/glasses — e.g., "커피 한 잔, 물 두 잔" (*One cup of coffee, two glasses of water*)
  - [ ] `개` (gae, Hanja: 個) [Counter]: general counter for objects/items — e.g., "사과 세 개 주세요." (*Please give me three apples.*)
  - [ ] `하나 / 둘 / 셋 / 넷` (hana / dul / set / net) [Noun]: native Korean numbers 1, 2, 3, 4 — e.g., "Before counters: 한 개, 두 개, 세 개, 네 개." (*1 item, 2 items, 3 items, 4 items.*)
  - [ ] `주세요` (juseyo) [Expression]: please give me — e.g., "메뉴판 좀 주세요." (*Please give me the menu.*)

#### Module 5.1.3 — 문법 (Grammar Formulas & Rules)

- [ ] **Grammar Pattern: `Noun + (Number + Counter) + 주세요`** (Please give me [X amount of Noun])
  - Formula: `[Item Noun] + [Native Number modifier] + [Counter] + 주세요`
  - Explanation: When counting items in Korean, Native Korean numbers (하나, 둘, 셋, 넷, 다섯...) undergo a phonetic shortening before counters: 하나 -> 한, 둘 -> 두, 셋 -> 세, 넷 -> 네, 스물 -> 스무.
  - Rule: 커피 1잔: 커피 한 잔 주세요 (NOT 하나 잔)
  - Rule: 물 2잔: 물 두 잔 주세요 (NOT 둘 잔)
  - Rule: 케이크 3개: 케이크 세 개 주세요 (NOT 셋 개)
  - Rule: 빵 4개: 빵 네 개 주세요 (NOT 넷 개)
  - Example: `아이스 아메리카노 한 잔 주세요.` — *Please give me one iced Americano.* [아이스 아메리카노 + 한 (1) + 잔 (cup) + 주세요 (give me please)]
  - Example: `쿠키 두 개 주세요.` — *Please give me two cookies.* [쿠키 (cookies) + 두 (2) + 개 (counter) + 주세요]

#### Module 5.1.4 — 말하기·듣기 (Shadowing & Drills)

- [ ] **Shadowing & Repetition Drill:** Shadow each sentence and practice substituting drink orders and quantities smoothly.
  - [ ] Prompt: "Order 1 hot green tea." → Speak aloud: `따뜻한 녹차 한 잔 주세요.` (*Please give me one hot green tea.*)
  - [ ] Prompt: "Order 2 iced coffees." → Speak aloud: `아이스 커피 두 잔 주세요.` (*Please give me two iced coffees.*)
  - [ ] Prompt: "Order 3 glasses of water." → Speak aloud: `물 세 잔 주세요.` (*Please give me three glasses of water.*)
  - [ ] Prompt: "Order 1 sandwich to go." → Speak aloud: `샌드위치 하나 포장해 주세요.` (*Please wrap one sandwich to go.*)

#### Module 5.1.5 — 읽기·쓰기 (Reading & Production)

- [ ] **Reading Passage:** "카페 메뉴판 (Cafe Menu Receipt)"
  - Text: `서울카페 영수증 -------------------- 아메리카노 (Ice)  2잔  9,000원 카페라떼 (Hot)   1잔  5,000원 초코 머핀         1개  3,500원 -------------------- 합계: 17,500원 결제 완료 (신용카드)`
  - Translation: *Seoul Cafe Receipt -------------------- Americano (Ice) 2 cups 9,000 KRW Cafe Latte (Hot) 1 cup 5,000 KRW Chocolate Muffin 1 item 3,500 KRW -------------------- Total: 17,500 KRW Payment Complete (Credit Card)*
  - [ ] Check: How many cups of iced Americano were ordered? → Answer: `2잔 (2 cups)` (*아메리카노 (Ice) 2잔*)
  - [ ] Check: What is the total price on the receipt? → Answer: `17,500원 (17,500 KRW)` (*합계: 17,500원*)
- [ ] **Guided Writing Task:** Write a realistic 4-line cafe ordering dialogue between a barista and yourself.
  - Sample Model Answer: `점원: 어서 오세요! 주문하시겠어요? 나: 네, 따뜻한 카페라떼 한 잔 주세요. 점원: 더 필요한 것 있으세요? 나: 그리고 딸기 케이크 하나 주세요. 감사합니다.`

### 5.2 — Ordering Food at a Restaurant (식당에서 음식 주문하기)

**Situation & Objective.** Order Korean meals using the object particle 을/를 and connect items with 하고 and 와/과.

#### Module 5.2.1 — 대화 (Conversation First)

- [ ] **Dialogue Practice:** Listen to and practice the dialogue between Min-su (민수) and Sarah (사라) and Min-su (민수) and Server (식당 이모님) and Min-su (민수).
  - `사라 씨, 한국 음식 좋아해요? 뭐 먹을래요?` (Sarah, do you like Korean food? What would you like to eat?) — *Note: 뭐 먹을래요? = "What would you like to eat?"*
  - `저는 비빔밥을 정말 좋아해요. 불고기도 좋아해요.` (I really like bibimbap. I also like bulgogi.) — *Note: 을/를 marks the grammatical direct object of liking/eating.*
  - `여기요! 비빔밥 하나하고 불고기 일 인분 주세요.` (Over here! Please give us one bibimbap and one serving of bulgogi.) — *Note: 여기요 = Polite call to restaurant staff; 인분 = portion counter.*
  - `네! 찌개도 드릴까요? 김치찌개와 된장찌개가 맛있어요.` (Yes! Shall I give you stew too? The kimchi stew and soybean paste stew are delicious.) — *Note: 와/과 = formal/written "and"; 찌개 = stew.*
  - `된장찌개 하나 주세요. 그리고 시원한 물도 주세요.` (Please give us one soybean paste stew. And please give us cold water too.) — *Note: 하나 주세요 = "Give us one please".*
- [ ] **Cultural Etiquette & Pragmatics:** In Korean casual restaurants (분식집, 한식당), there are usually no table bells or servers hovering. To call the server politely, raise your hand slightly and say "여기요!" (Over here!) or "저기요!" (Excuse me over there!). Water and side dishes (반찬) are frequently self-service ("물은 셀프입니다").

#### Module 5.2.2 — 어휘 (Vocabulary in Context)

- [ ] **Core Target Words:** Memorise and pronounce all target words for this lesson:
  - [ ] `밥 / 쌀밥` (bap / ssalbap) [Noun]: cooked rice / meal — e.g., "밥을 맛있게 먹어요." (*I eat the meal deliciously.*)
  - [ ] `비빔밥` (bibimbap) [Noun]: bibimbap (mixed rice with vegetables and meat) — e.g., "전주 비빔밥을 제일 좋아해요." (*I like Jeonju bibimbap the most.*)
  - [ ] `불고기` (bulgogi) [Noun]: bulgogi (marinated grilled beef) — e.g., "불고기 이 인분 주세요." (*Please give us two servings of bulgogi.*)
  - [ ] `김치찌개` (gimchijjigae) [Noun]: kimchi stew — e.g., "김치찌개가 조금 매워요." (*The kimchi stew is a little spicy.*)
  - [ ] `된장찌개` (doenjangjjigae) [Noun]: soybean paste stew — e.g., "된장찌개가 아주 구수해요." (*The soybean paste stew is very rich and savory.*)
  - [ ] `하고 / 와 / 과` (hago / wa / gwa) [Particle]: and / with (connecting nouns) — e.g., "비빔밥하고 콜라 주세요." (*Please give me bibimbap and cola.*)
  - [ ] `인분` (inbun, Hanja: 人份) [Counter]: counter for food portions/servings (uses Sino-Korean numbers) — e.g., "삼겹살 2인분 (이 인분) 주세요." (*Please give us 2 servings of pork belly.*)

#### Module 5.2.3 — 문법 (Grammar Formulas & Rules)

- [ ] **Grammar Pattern: `Noun + 을/를`** (Object particle (marks the direct object of a transitive verb))
  - Formula: `Consonant ends + 을 | Vowel ends + 를`
  - Explanation: The object particle attaches to the receiver of the action (eating, buying, studying, drinking).
  - Rule: Noun ends in a consonant (받침 O): 밥 -> 밥을, 물 -> 물을, 책 -> 책을
  - Rule: Noun ends in a vowel (받침 X): 커피 -> 커피를, 사과 -> 사과를, 불고기 -> 불고기를
